# Impact of Blockchain-Enabled IoT Applications for Smart Agriculture and Healthcare to Promote Sustainable Economic Growth and Smart Health Management Ecosystem in Industry 5.0

  
  
**Bhavneet Kaur Sachdev^([1](#9789815305210-C5.html#aff1))****,** **Sumanta Bhattacharya^([2](#9789815305210-C5.html#aff2), [\*](#9789815305210-C5.html#cor1))**  
¹ Calcutta University, Kolkata, India  
² Maulana Abul Kalam Azad University of Technology, Kolkata, West Bengal, India  
  

## Abstract

Within the framework of Industry 5.0, the incorporation of blockchain-enabled IoT applications into smart agriculture and healthcare has significant consequences for long-term economic growth and the creation of ecosystems for smart health management. The environmental, economic, industrial, and stakeholder implications of smart agriculture's transparent supply chains, which are made possible by blockchain technology and precision farming techniques, lessen the environmental impact of conventional farming by cutting down on resource use. Blockchain technology and Internet of Things (IoT) devices safeguard patient records, allowing for more eco-friendly procedures with less paper waste. These innovations maximize the use of agricultural resources, which boosts output while decreasing expenditures from an economic perspective. Better patient outcomes and lower healthcare costs are possible because of blockchain technology's assurance of data integrity and interoperability. This promotes a fairer and more inclusive economic climate, giving smaller players a voice. Innovation and integration are propelled by the industrial sector. By fostering an environment of open communication and cooperation, the tenets of Industry 5.0 aim to bring together many sectors of the economy to improve technological standards, strengthen cybersecurity, and standardize procedures. Awareness, training, and community participation are all made easier by stakeholders, which include NGOs. For these technologies to be adopted in an ethical and responsible manner, their advocacy for legislation that supports them and the protection of data ownership rights are vital. In the future, there will be more international cooperation, new technological solutions to problems with energy efficiency, and the creation of universal benchmarks.

**Keywords:** Agricultural resources, Advocacy, Blockchain, Cybersecurity, Community participation, Data integrity, Ecosystem, Eco-friendly, Economic perspective, Environment impact, Healthcare cost, Industry 5.0, International cooperation, Internet of Things, Paper waste, NGOs, Smart health management, Stakeholders, Smart agriculture, Technology.  
  

* * *

^(\*) **Corresponding author Sumanta Bhattacharya:** Maulana Abul Kalam Azad University of Technology, Kolkata, West Bengal, India; E-mail: [sumanta.21394@gmail.com](mailto:sumanta.21394@gmail.com%20)  
  

## INTRODUCTION

Blockchain-enabled Internet of Things (IoT) applications have the potential to revolutionise smart agriculture and healthcare. Blockchain and Internet of Things applications can improve supply chain visibility and auditability in the context of smart agriculture. Data from Internet of Things (IoT) sensors, such as those tracking soil moisture, temperature, humidity, and crop health, can be recorded and stored securely on a distributed ledger, giving stakeholders confidence in the reliability of data pertaining to agricultural practises and products. This not only promotes sustainable and ethical agricultural practises but also minimises the possibility of fraud by providing consumers with verified information about the origin and quality of agricultural products. Optimising resource utilisation is one way in which the adoption of blockchain and IoT in agriculture promotes long-term economic prosperity \[[1](#9789815305210-C5.html#r1)\]. To guarantee that farmers are fairly compensated for their goods, smart contracts on the blockchain can streamline transactions and legal agreements. Increased agricultural output and profits may result from the greater ease and openness of financial dealings. Moreover, the information gathered by IoT devices may be used for data-driven decision-making, allowing farmers to implement precision agriculture practises that boost productivity while reducing environmental impact. A smart health management ecosystem can be established when blockchain technology is combined with the Internet of Things. Patient data obtained through IoT devices, such as wearable health monitors, can be securely kept on a blockchain. The confidentiality of patient information is protected while open communication between medical professionals is facilitated. Increased patient agency over their own health information supports care delivery centered on the individual. Automating procedures like insurance claims with blockchain-based smart contracts can cut down on paperwork and boost the healthcare system's productivity. Human-centered design and cutting-edge tech go hand in hand in the “Industry 5.0” paradigm shift. In this light, IoT applications enabled by blockchain are congruent with the goal of establishing interdependent ecosystems that put an emphasis on efficiency and sustainability. Blockchain transactions are transparent and safe, so everyone involved in agriculture and healthcare can trust the data being shared between their connected equipment \[[2](#9789815305210-C5.html#r2)\]. Industry 5.0 is characterised by the rise of cooperative, networked systems that are capable of enhancing themselves. Blockchain's increased security and privacy is a major selling point when applied to the Internet of Things. Sensitive data is generated and transferred in both smart agriculture and healthcare. The distributed ledger and cryptographic properties of blockchain technology guarantee that information cannot be altered and that only authorised users can access it. This not only promotes good data management practises but also protects the privacy of individuals' health information throughout the agriculture supply chain. It has a significant impact on smart agriculture and healthcare, fostering the growth of sustainable economies and new smart health management ecosystems \[[3](#9789815305210-C5.html#r3)\]. As Industry 5.0 develops further, these technologies provide novel answers for the administration of agricultural operations and healthcare systems, with an emphasis on efficiency, transparency, and security.

## Environment Consideration

There are major environmental issues with smart agricultural and healthcare systems that include blockchain and the IoT. In order to solve environmental problems, technical progress in these areas must be in line with sustainability objectives, making these factors critical. The Internet of Things (IoT) allows for the improvement of resource usage in smart agriculture through the use of devices like sensors, drones, and automated machinery. Because of this, farmers are able to use less water, fertiliser, and pesticide, leading to more efficient practices overall. This helps with sustainable farming and resource conservation by reducing the negative impact of conventional farming practices on the environment. The use of blockchain technology in smart agriculture improves eco-friendliness by creating a verifiable and transparent supply chain. By being open and honest about where their food comes from, consumers can rest certain that their food is not part of any unlawful or unsustainable methods that harm the environment \[[4](#9789815305210-C5.html#r4)\]. With this information at their fingertips, shoppers can demand more sustainable agricultural techniques that are good for the environment. Blockchain and the Internet of Things (IoT) can also simplify healthcare operations, which in turn reduces the need for resources and the amount of waste produced. There will be less need for paper records thanks to digital health records kept on blockchain systems, which means less paper waste and less pollution. Wearable monitors and remote patient monitoring tools are examples of Internet of Things (IoT) gadgets that are finding use in healthcare. These devices allow for early detection and preventative interventions, which in turn reduce the environmental effect of emergency treatments and hospitalisations. By reducing energy waste and making better use of healthcare resources, this new proactive approach is environmentally friendly \[[5](#9789815305210-C5.html#r5)\]. Health data stored on blockchain systems is decentralised and secure, which means less need for physical infrastructure like huge data centres. This means less energy use and a smaller carbon impact. With more and more healthcare systems turning to digital solutions, this is becoming more important, especially because people are starting to worry about the effects of data storage on the environment. If blockchain and IoT are serious about reducing their negative effects on the environment, they must be designed to use less energy. To make sure that the advantages of these technologies do not come at the expense of increasing energy consumption and carbon emissions, innovations in low-power IoT devices and consensus procedures in blockchain networks are crucial. Another important factor to think about is how to properly dispose of the electrical garbage that is produced by old Internet of Things devices. To lessen the toll that electronic waste has on the environment and to forestall contamination from toxic substances, producers should make recycling and responsible disposal their top priorities \[[6](#9789815305210-C5.html#r6)\]. Thorough environmental impact studies should precede the use of blockchain and IoT technologies in order to detect and mitigate any adverse effects. By taking this preventative measure, we can make sure that these technologies have the most positive impact on smart agriculture and healthcare while having the least negative impact on the environment. There must be a concerted effort to inform the public of the environmental advantages of blockchain and the Internet of Things (IoT) in smart farming and healthcare. Showcasing the beneficial environmental effect of these technologies and encouraging stakeholders to adopt sustainable behaviors may promote a collective commitment to ecologically responsible use. A firm dedication to environmental sustainability should govern the deployment of blockchain and the Internet of Things, notwithstanding the revolutionary potential they hold in smart healthcare and agriculture. If we want our technical innovations to have a beneficial and long-lasting effect on the environment, we must find a way to balance them \[[7](#9789815305210-C5.html#r7)\]. Fig. (**1**) explains the impact of blockchain and IoT on environment.

![](Images/9789815305210-C5-F1.jpg)**Fig. (1))**  
Impact of Blockchain and IoT on the Environment. 

## Blockchain and IoT Contribution to Economic Growth

In the context of Industry 5.0, blockchain-enabled IoT applications might revolutionise smart agricultural and healthcare systems, which in turn could create significant economic development. By maximising efficiency and savviness with resources through the provision of real-time data on crop conditions, soil health, and weather patterns, blockchain and IoT technologies can improve the efficiency of smart agriculture. More agricultural output means more money in the economy, and this data-driven strategy helps farmers do just that by making better use of their resources and wasting less. Advanced solutions like sharding, sidechains *etc.* enable the effective handling of voluminous data related to smart agriculture and healthcare services. Layer 2 protocols, like ARCnet, Asynchronous Transfer Mode, Econet, Ethernet, Ethernet Automatic Protection Switching, Fiber Distributed Data Interface, Frame relay, High-level Data Link Control, Lightning Network, *etc.*, enable the efficient transfer of data in a wide area network \[[4](#9789815305210-C5.html#r4)\]. The impact of technology has been depicted in Fig. (**2**).

![](Images/9789815305210-C5-F2.jpg)**Fig. (2))**  
Technological contribution to economic growth. 

### Encouraging Openness and Traceability in the Supply Chain

Smart agriculture uses blockchain technology to make the supply chain more transparent and traceable. This improves the quality of agricultural goods while also decreasing the possibility of fraud. Businesses may boost their income and provide economic advantages to stakeholders by charging premium rates for their products when their supply chain is more trustworthy.

Spreading Power and Encouraging Participation: Due to blockchain's decentralised structure, smaller players in smart agriculture are given more influence. Because of this openness, formerly marginalised places might see economic growth as smaller farmers are able to take part in a global marketplace.

### Healthcare Decision-Making Based on Data

Decisions in healthcare may now be based on data thanks to the integration of blockchain and the Internet of Things \[[8](#9789815305210-C5.html#r8)\]. As a result, healthcare outcomes, including diagnostic accuracy and personalized treatment strategies, are improved. The economic effect rests on the possible reduction in healthcare costs through preventative measures and early treatments, leading to a more sustainable healthcare system.

### Reduced Expenses and Enhanced Efficiency

Internet of Things (IoT) devices and blockchain enable smart agriculture and healthcare to automate and streamline procedures, resulting in substantial cost savings. By streamlining processes, cutting down on red tape, and increasing operational efficiency, we can cut out the middlemen and put more money into the economy.

### Simplified Obtaining Funds

When it comes to smart agriculture, blockchain technology allows for safe and transparent financial transactions. Farmers' creditworthiness is boosted by this, in addition to real-time data on crop success. The agricultural industry contributes significantly to economic growth because better investment in technology and infrastructure is made possible by improved access to finance \[[9](#9789815305210-C5.html#r9)\].

### Working Together and Interoperability

Collaboration in Industry 5.0 is enhanced by the interoperability of blockchain and IoT technologies. What this implies for smart agriculture is that all the various systems and gadgets can communicate with one another without a hitch. It makes it possible for different parties in the healthcare industry to safely share patient data with one another. Because of the pooling of resources and expertise, new ideas are born, and the economy expands as a result of such partnerships. Global trade in agriculture and healthcare is facilitated by blockchain's trust and openness in the supply chain. With self-assurance, healthcare providers and farmers may join global marketplaces, increasing their impact and boosting economies everywhere \[[10](#9789815305210-C5.html#r10)\]. Smart contracts that promote sustainable practices in healthcare and agriculture can be integrated into blockchain-enabled systems. Incentives or premium rates should be offered to farmers and healthcare professionals who embrace eco-friendly and ethical methods, encouraging both economic development and sustainability. Data analytics, cybersecurity, and blockchain developers are in high demand as more and more Internet of Things (IoT) applications use blockchain technology. As a result, the changing face of Industry 5.0 helps to foster economic growth by creating jobs and improving people's abilities. In the age of Industry 5.0, the synergies between blockchain and Internet of Things applications in smart agriculture and healthcare greatly contribute to economic growth by improving transparency, efficiency, and cooperation. They also spur innovation and inclusive development \[[11](#9789815305210-C5.html#r11)\].

## Social Impact on Farmers and Healthcare Sector

Smart agriculture and healthcare that uses blockchain and the IoT have far-reaching societal effects, especially on farmers and the healthcare industry as a whole. Fig. (**3**) depicts the impact of smart agriculture on society. There are far-reaching consequences on people's and communities' well-being brought about by these technologies, which boost efficiency and production. By giving them access to trustworthy digital platforms, blockchain-enabled smart agriculture gives smallholder farmers more agency. Because of this inclusion, they are able to have a bigger say in the agricultural ecosystem, participate more effectively in marketplaces, and get fair pay for their goods. Smart agriculture, which makes use of blockchain technology and the Internet of Things, helps farmers maximise their harvests by reducing waste \[[12](#9789815305210-C5.html#r12)\]. Consequently, this results in higher incomes and better living conditions. Improved agricultural efficiency would allow farmers to put more money into their communities and provide a better life for their families.

Better access to financial services for farmers is made possible by blockchain's transparent and traceable nature. It becomes much simpler to acquire loans and other financial instruments for investments in agriculture when they can prove their trustworthiness using smart contracts and real-time data on crop conditions. Blockchain and the Internet of Things bring farmers together, strengthening their bonds as a community. As a result of farmers sharing information on things like weather, pests, and crop performance, agricultural communities are better equipped to work together to solve problems and learn from one another \[[13](#9789815305210-C5.html#r13)\]. Blockchain and the Internet of Things (IoT) can make healthcare more accessible. By facilitating communication between healthcare practitioners and patients in underserved or far-flung locations, technologies like remote monitoring and telemedicine help those who might otherwise have trouble getting the treatment they need when they need it. The use of blockchain technology in healthcare guarantees the privacy, integrity, and ownership of patient records. By putting the patient first, this method encourages open communication and cooperation between doctors and their patients by giving them greater agency over their health records. By integrating blockchain technology with IoT devices in healthcare, health metrics may be continuously monitored. This makes it easier to implement preventative healthcare measures, as problems may be detected sooner, which improves health in general and lowers healthcare expenses \[[14](#9789815305210-C5.html#r14)\]. Protecting the confidentiality of medical and agricultural records is what blockchain technology is all about. People can trust that their medical information will be kept secure, and farmers may communicate data about agricultural conditions without worrying about it being manipulated. As a result, a feeling of safety and reliability is fostered in these areas.

![](Images/9789815305210-C5-F3.jpg)**Fig. (3))**  
Impact of smart agriculture and healthcare on society. 

The social benefit of smart agriculture also includes environmental care, which brings us to our ninth point. Farmers can make the world a better place by improving environmental conditions with the use of Internet of Things (IoT) devices to optimise resource utilisation and blockchain technology to ensure sustainable practices. Education and skill development are essential for the adoption of blockchain and the Internet of Things. Knowledge of how to use technology, understand data, and manage systems is useful for both farmers and medical professionals \[[15](#9789815305210-C5.html#r15)\]. In addition to enhancing their own abilities, this helps communities economically and socially as a whole. The Internet of Things (IoT) and blockchain technology have far-reaching societal implications for smart agriculture and healthcare. These technologies are crucial in bringing about beneficial social change in several areas, such as healthcare (by making it more accessible and increasing patient outcomes) and agriculture (by giving farmers more control over their livelihoods).

The advancement of blockchain identity management will mitigate the risk of identity theft. The technologies like self-sovereign identity framework, decentralized identifiers, verifiable credentials, *etc.*, empower individuals to attain complete control over their personal data in the field of agriculture and the healthcare system.

## Role of the Industrial Sector in Sustainable Development

When it comes to smart agriculture and healthcare, the industrial sector is crucial for making the most of blockchain-enabled Internet of Things applications, particularly within the context of Industry 5.0. Industrial actors may take advantage of this revolutionary convergence of technologies to boost efficiency, promote innovation, and contribute to sustainable development. Industrial firms are leading the charge for smart agricultural innovation through blockchain and IoT integration. They put money into research and development of cutting-edge technology, including sensor networks, automated machinery, and blockchain-enabled systems, to improve farming operations \[[16](#9789815305210-C5.html#r16)\]. The industrial sector can optimise the whole agricultural supply chain by leveraging blockchain technology and its huge supply chain networks. Agricultural product production, processing, and distribution may all be monitored and verified in this way. Distributed ledger technology (blockchain) improves supply chain accountability and quality control by making transactions transparent and traceable. Fig. (**4**) denotes contribution of Industrial sector in sustainable economic growth and development.

Industry 5.0 places an emphasis on the interdependence of different systems. With the help of industrial players, smart agricultural and healthcare ecosystems may integrate varied components, allowing for smooth connections between blockchain networks and IoT devices. The overall performance and efficiency of the system are improved by these interconnections. Data management and analysis are areas where the industrial sector thrives. Industrial actors can build strong analytics platforms using the vast quantities of data produced by IoT devices in healthcare and agriculture. By guaranteeing the data's confidentiality and integrity, blockchain lays a solid groundwork for well-informed decisions. Industrial players may help with the process of standardising protocols and interfaces in applications that use blockchain technology to enable the Internet of Things \[[17](#9789815305210-C5.html#r17)\]. With these standards in place, smart agriculture and healthcare may more easily integrate various devices and systems, creating an ecosystem that is more coherent and interoperable. Improving efficiency is the core focus of smart manufacturing processes in Industry 5.0. By incorporating blockchain and IoT technology into production processes, the industrial sector can guarantee that production data is transparent and intact. This, in turn, helps in the manufacturing of dependable and trackable farm and medical apparatus.

![](Images/9789815305210-C5-F4.jpg)**Fig. (4))**  
Contribution of the industrial sector to sustainable economic growth and development. 

Industrial players have the financial means to pour a lot of money into R&D projects pertaining to blockchain and Internet of Things (IoT) applications. For smart agriculture and healthcare, this means creating specialised sensors, communication protocols, and blockchain solutions. Healthcare and agriculture data are particularly sensitive; thus, it is crucial that the industrial sector takes the lead in establishing strong cybersecurity safeguards. To ensure the privacy and security of data in ecosystems enabled by blockchain technology, it is necessary to design secure communication routes, encryption mechanisms, and access control systems. Companies in the industrial sector may encourage partnerships with blockchain and Internet of Things (IoT) innovators and startups \[[18](#9789815305210-C5.html#r18)\]. Larger industrial corporations may benefit from new ideas and technology for smart agriculture and healthcare by teaming up with smaller, more nimble businesses. Smart agriculture and healthcare are two areas where the industrial sector may promote and execute sustainable practices. The agriculture sector can do its part to improve environmental and social conditions by adopting more sustainable production practices and using blockchain technology to track the origin of goods. The success of Industry 5.0 hinges on the industrial sector's ability to utilise blockchain-enabled Internet of Things applications for smart agriculture and healthcare. The successful and long-term incorporation of these technologies into contemporary healthcare and agriculture depends on industry actors driving positive transitions through standardisation, collaboration, and innovation \[[8](#9789815305210-C5.html#r8)\].

## Stakeholders and NGOs’ Interest

In order to successfully deploy and manage smart agriculture, which integrates blockchain and the Internet of Things (IoT), stakeholders and non-governmental organisations (NGOs) are vital. Their participation is critical for the agriculture industry to tackle many issues, promote inclusivity, and implement sustainable methods. Fig. (**5**) shows the roles of stakeholder in promotion Blockchain and IoT usage in agriculture and health care ecosystem. By bringing attention to the issue among farmers, legislators, and community members, NGOs and other interested parties may push for the integration of blockchain and IoT into smart agriculture. Their work in spreading the word about these technologies' advantages, building awareness, and calming fears is crucial. Non-governmental organisations (NGOs) may help farmers learn how to use blockchain and the Internet of Things (IoT) by organising training programmes and capacity-building projects. Part of this is training people to set up and operate smart devices so farmers can make data-driven decisions \[[19](#9789815305210-C5.html#r19)\]. In order to help farmers, particularly smallholders, access capital and resources for smart agricultural solutions, stakeholders and NGOs may play a key role. To make sure that the financial hurdles to adopting new technologies are minimised, this support might take the form of grants, subsidies, or collaborations with financial institutions. It is crucial to involve local communities and farmers in the decision-making processes when it comes to smart agriculture implementation. By mediating between different parties, NGOs may make sure that everyone's opinions are taken into consideration and that technological solutions are tailored to meet the requirements of the community. When it comes to integrating blockchain and the Internet of Things (IoT) in agriculture, non-governmental organisations (NGOs) may help set standards and best practices. The constant use of technology across diverse areas and agricultural methods is ensured by this framework for uniformity and interoperability \[[20](#9789815305210-C5.html#r20)\].

Sustainable agriculture techniques may be advanced by stakeholders and non-governmental organisations. Some examples of this include pushing for more transparent and environmentally friendly supply chains made possible by blockchain technology and promoting the use of precision farming methods made possible by Internet of Things devices to reduce resource use. When it comes to promoting policies that help, NGOs are crucial on a global, national, and even local scale. Together with governments, they can solve data privacy and security issues and promote the proper use of blockchain and the Internet of Things (IoT) in agriculture \[[4](#9789815305210-C5.html#r4)\]. To ensure the success of smart agricultural initiatives, ongoing monitoring and assessment are crucial. In order to make these programmes more effective overall, stakeholders and NGOs may help by performing impact evaluations, finding ways to improve them, and sharing what they have learned. When it comes to smart agriculture, NGOs may fight for farmers' rights to data ownership and privacy protection. They can try to put systems in place that give farmers more say over their data and make them more transparent about its use in the blockchain ecosystem. It is essential for NGOs, stakeholders, and other players in the agricultural value chain to collaborate with one another. Together, these groups can better manage blockchain and Internet of Things (IoT) solutions for smart agriculture by combining their resources and knowledge.

![](Images/9789815305210-C5-F5.jpg)**Fig. (5))**  
Stakeholder’s role in the promotion of blockchain and IoT usage in the agriculture and healthcare ecosystem. 

When it comes to healthcare, stakeholders and NGOs are the ones that push for the use of blockchain and the Internet of Things. They are crucial in getting the word out about these technologies' potential advantages to healthcare practitioners, lawmakers, and the public at large. Blockchain and the Internet of Things (IoT) have the potential to revolutionise healthcare administration, and they are working to make these ideas more accessible through a variety of educational initiatives. Stakeholders and non-governmental organisations (NGOs) work together to create rules and guidelines for healthcare blockchain and IoT integration. Addressing issues with data privacy, security, and ethics, they collaborate with government agencies to establish a regulatory climate that is favourable to their business. Their insightful comments help shape policies that will govern the ethical and responsible use of these technologies. New healthcare initiatives frequently receive funding and other resources from NGOs. Blockchain and Internet of Things (IoT) application development and implementation may be the focus of their funding efforts \[[21](#9789815305210-C5.html#r21)\]. The testing and scaling of new technologies within the healthcare industry is made easier with this financial assistance, which helps to overcome financing shortages. Smart healthcare programmes are continuously monitored and evaluated with the help of non-governmental organisations and other stakeholders. Working in tandem with healthcare practitioners, tech developers, and academics, they evaluate how blockchain and IoT technologies affect healthcare delivery, patient outcomes, and system efficiency as a whole. Technology may be fine-tuned and improved through this feedback loop so that it can adapt to the changing healthcare scenario.

## Role of Policies and Governance

When it comes to smart agriculture and health management, data security and privacy are major considerations. Policies are key in developing legal frameworks to address these concerns. Due to the delicate nature of agricultural and healthcare data, strict restrictions are necessary to establish confidence among all parties involved. In order to promote the safe and ethical usage of blockchain and IoT technologies, governance systems make sure that these principles are followed. Fig. (**6**) depicts the policies and governance required for smart farming management.

To make sure that various blockchain and IoT systems can communicate and integrate with one other without any hitches, policies can establish interoperability standards. A more cohesive and interdependent ecosystem may be achieved by standardisation, which allows for cooperation among many stakeholders. Reducing compatibility difficulties and fostering innovation across the sector, well-defined standards help facilitate the adoption of technology. Sustainable agriculture and healthcare may be encouraged by government policy. Green and socially responsible farming and healthcare may be incentivized by lawmakers through the use of blockchain and Internet of Things technologies \[[22](#9789815305210-C5.html#r22)\]. To ensure these industries' continued viability in the long run, governments often provide financial incentives like tax breaks, subsidies, or special treatment in the marketplace. Data created by Internet of Things devices and kept on blockchain systems raises questions of ownership and permission, which should be addressed by policies. Patients are given more agency over their health records when there are explicit rules about who owns what in the healthcare system. To make sure people know what will happen to their data and how it will be used, governance systems make sure consent is gained in a transparent way. By removing barriers across fields like agriculture and healthcare, policies may encourage collaboration across sectors. Industry 5.0 is all about integrating technology, and it's not just in one industry. Synergies between smart agriculture and health management may be fostered by government regulations that support joint projects \[[23](#9789815305210-C5.html#r23)\]. This will lead to complete and linked solutions.

![](Images/9789815305210-C5-F6.jpg)**Fig. (6))**  
Policies and governance for smart farming management. 

When it comes to fostering the growth of the digital infrastructure essential to the broad use of blockchain and IoT technologies, governments can be pivotal. Part of this investment strategy involves the construction of data centers, high-speed internet connections, and other forms of technical infrastructure. Advancements in smart agriculture and health management can be facilitated by policies that encourage these investments. The application of blockchain technology and the Internet of Things in healthcare and agriculture raises important ethical questions that should be addressed by policymakers. By establishing and enforcing norms for responsible innovation, governance systems may make sure that digital progress always puts ethics, human rights, and society's welfare first. By taking this tack, we may encourage the creation of tech that conforms to people's norms and expectations \[[22](#9789815305210-C5.html#r22)\]. To guarantee that those involved in healthcare and agriculture have the knowledge and abilities to utilise blockchain and IoT technology, policies might focus on education and capacity building. Farmers, healthcare providers, and lawmakers may all benefit from government-funded training programmes, seminars, and educational efforts that help them comprehend and make good use of these technologies. When it comes to smart agriculture and health management, regulations and governance play a crucial role in establishing a climate that encourages innovation, safeguards data, and supports long-term economic growth in the age of Industry 5.0. The responsible and ethical integration of blockchain and IoT technology into these vital industries can only be achieved with well-designed rules and strong governance systems.

## Challenges

Concerns about standardisation and compatibility arise from the integration of several blockchain systems with IoT devices as shown in Fig. (**7**). Standardised protocols are necessary for smart agriculture and healthcare to achieve smooth connection and data sharing across diverse devices and systems. These technologies may not be able to be widely used or used effectively due to a lack of compatibility. A major obstacle to Industry 5.0's emphasis on system interconnection is scalability. Achieving long-term development requires making sure that blockchain networks and Internet of Things apps can manage the massive amounts of data produced by smart agriculture and healthcare. To meet the growing breadth and depth of Industry 5.0 settings, scalability issues must be resolved.

Health and agricultural data are very sensitive, thus strong privacy and security safeguards are needed. There are still obstacles to assuring the protection of personal and private information using blockchain despite its reputation for security. If we want people to have faith in these systems, we need to address their worries about data breaches, illegal access, and protecting their health information \[[24](#9789815305210-C5.html#r24)\]. Many questions remain about how healthcare and agriculture will be regulated in relation to blockchain and the Internet of Things. The problem lies in creating new policies that can accept these novel technologies while yet adhering to existing restrictions. To guarantee that these applications adhere to legal and ethical norms, it is crucial to find a happy medium between innovation and regulatory compliance. Smart farms and hospitals will have to fork out a pretty penny to start using blockchain and IoT. In particular, smaller stakeholders may find the expenditure necessary for infrastructure, device deployment, and system integration to be prohibitive. For these technologies to be used sustainably, it is essential to address concerns about their return on investment while simultaneously encouraging investment in them. A workforce that can comprehend and oversee applications of blockchain and the Internet of Things is essential for Industry 5.0. To guarantee the efficient usage and upkeep of these technologies, it is difficult to educate and train farmers, healthcare providers, and IT staff adequately. In order to fully realise the advantages of Industry 5.0, it is crucial to close the talent gap. In industries with long-standing norms, new technology adoption is more likely to encounter pushback. Traditional processes may face resistance from stakeholders such as farmers and healthcare professionals \[[25](#9789815305210-C5.html#r25)\]. In order to successfully integrate blockchain-enabled Internet of Things applications in smart agriculture and healthcare, it is essential to overcome this reluctance and promote a culture of creativity. Blockchain technology, particularly its consensus method known as proof-of-work, can consume a lot of power. Considering the importance of environmental sustainability in Industry 5.0, it is crucial to tackle the energy consumption linked to blockchain. To lessen the impact of this problem, researchers could look at different blockchain systems or create consensus processes that use less energy. It can be difficult to integrate blockchain and IoT technology into preexisting systems. The trick is to make sure everything goes well without interrupting current activities. Planning, cooperation, and staggered deployment may be necessary due to the complexity of integration in order to minimise interruptions and maximise efficiency. One difficulty with blockchain-enabled systems is the lack of consensus on data ownership and governance structures. There are new factors to think about when deciding who owns what data, thanks to smart contracts and decentralised control systems. For growth that is both sustainable and equitable, it is vital to develop frameworks that balance the rights of individuals to own data with the advantages of sharing data collectively. Developers of technology, lawmakers, business moguls, and others must work together to solve these problems. In order to achieve Industry 5.0's goals of smart health management and sustainable economic growth, it is necessary to remove these barriers before blockchain-enabled Internet of Things (IoT) applications in smart agriculture and healthcare can reach their maximum potential \[[3](#9789815305210-C5.html#r3)\].

## Future perspectives

In the age of Industry 5.0, the smart health management ecosystem will be shaped by blockchain-enabled Internet of Things (IoT) applications in smart agriculture and healthcare, which have the potential to promote sustainable economic growth. Smart agriculture and healthcare stakeholders will be able to share and collaborate more data because of blockchain's decentralised and transparent nature. The result will be a deeper understanding, which in turn will encourage teamwork in the ecosystem, allowing for collective data-driven decision-making that will boost efficiency and quality of life for patients. Blockchain technology is expected to be widely used in smart agriculture in the near future for improved supply chain tracking. The full supply chain of agricultural products will be transparently available to consumers, increasing the demand for items made in an ethical and environmentally responsible manner. Predictive analytics will be made possible in the healthcare and agricultural sectors through the combination of blockchain technology and artificial intelligence (AI). Both agricultural disease prediction and healthcare preventative medicine stand to benefit greatly from this. The insights that AI algorithms provide will help with proactive decision-making, and they are powered by massive datasets safely kept on blockchain systems. A new paradigm shift in agricultural ownership and investment may emerge as a result of tokenization, which is made possible by blockchain technology. One potential future direction is the tokenization of agricultural assets, which might open the door to fractional ownership and attract more investors interested in sustainable and creative farming methods. With blockchain-enabled solutions, patients will certainly have more say over their medical records. In the future, health management ecosystems may allow people to choose which healthcare providers, researchers, and other parties they share their health data with. This might lead to better healthcare results by putting the patient first.

![](Images/9789815305210-C5-F7.jpg)**Fig. (7))**  
Challenges faced by Blockchain and IoT technologies. 

Different advanced cryptographic techniques, *viz.*, symmetric key cryptography, public key cryptography, hash functions, digital signatures, multi-factor authentication, homomorphic encryption, the AES algorithm, RSA security encryption, asymmetric cryptography, *etc.*, add multiple layers to the security of sensitive data. These technologies will enable the secure processing of sensitive agricultural and healthcare data without compromising privacy.

To ensure privacy, effective performance, legal compliance, and usability in the application of blockchain and IoT systems, we need to develop the legal, organisational, semantic, and technical interoperability frameworks based on emerging standards like Hyperledger Fabric or Ethereum's Enterprise Ethereum Alliance (EEA) specifications.

We need to adopt open data standards and APIs (application programming interfaces) to facilitate seamless data sharing and interoperability among various stakeholders in smart agriculture and health management ecosystems.

It is anticipated that smart contracts will find more applications in smart agriculture, automating and securing a variety of agreements along the agricultural value chain. In the future, smart contracts might be used to reduce administrative burden and increase confidence in agreements concerning crop insurance, supply chain logistics, and equitable pay for farmers. More and more, blockchain and Internet of Things applications will use edge computing. By doing so, data may be processed in real-time right where it is generated, making smart agricultural and healthcare systems more responsive and lowering latency. Better and faster decisions may be made with the help of edge computing. Blockchain technology will pave the way for international R&D cooperation that is both safe and decentralised. With improved data sharing, scientists, researchers, and healthcare providers will be able to tackle agricultural problems and healthcare advances more quickly and collectively. We may anticipate that regulatory agencies will adapt to accommodate the novel applications of blockchain and the Internet of Things in healthcare and agriculture as these technologies develop further. In order to create an atmosphere that is favourable to the expansion of the sector, future plans involve the creation of more precise rules and regulations that strike a balance between innovation and privacy, security, and ethical concerns. Potentially more well-known are DAOs, which are made possible by blockchain technology. In smart agriculture, DAOs might help farmers make decisions together, and in healthcare, they could help with community-driven health programmes that follow the rules of Industry 5.0. Blockchain-enabled Internet of Things applications in smart healthcare and agriculture have promising futures. Health management ecosystems in the age of Industry 5.0 are anticipated to become more resilient and patient-centric as a result of these innovations, which are anticipated to modify conventional methods while also promoting sustainability. The implementation of smart contract auditing and advanced verification tools will facilitate the regular tracking of data privacy mechanisms and assess their compliance with the regulatory framework. Moreover, it will facilitate the continuous upgrade of the cyber privacy rules and interoperability framework.

## CONCLUSION

Industry 5.0 is about to undergo a dramatic shift as blockchain-enabled Internet of Things (IoT) applications in smart agriculture and healthcare create intelligent health management ecosystems and promote long-term economic growth. Through the integration of these technologies, precision farming is made possible, and responsible practices are promoted through increased openness, efficiency, and cooperation in the agricultural sector. The combination of real-time data from IoT devices with the safe and decentralised nature of blockchain paves the way for a patient-centric approach, preventative treatment, and enhanced overall well-being in healthcare. In the future, when Industry 5.0 takes shape, the combination of blockchain technology with the Internet of Things (IoT) is anticipated to cause a sea change in our approach to these vital areas, guaranteeing sustainability, inclusiveness, and resilience. Innovation, stakeholder engagement, and government cooperation hold the key to future technology breakthroughs and their revolutionary potential. To fully realise the promise of blockchain-enabled Internet of Things applications, future advancements should concentrate on solving problems like data security, legal frameworks, and interoperability. Smart agriculture and healthcare are leading the way in ethical and responsible innovation, which aligns with the concepts of Industry 5.0 and will bring about a new age where technology supports social well-being, environmental stewardship, and economic progress.

# Stride-Based Threat Modeling for Blockchain-Based Healthcare Supply Chain Management System

  
  
**S.V. Harish^([1](#9789815305210-C6.html#aff1))****,** **K. Chandrasekaran^([2](#9789815305210-C6.html#aff2), [\*](#9789815305210-C6.html#cor1))****,** **Rathnamma^([3](#9789815305210-C6.html#aff3))****,** **Usha Divakarla^([3](#9789815305210-C6.html#aff3))****,** **Venkata Ramana^([4](#9789815305210-C6.html#aff4))**  
¹ Manipal Institute of Technology, Manipal, Karnataka 576104, India  
² National Institute of Technology Karnataka, Surathkal, Mangalore, India  
³ KLM College of Engineering, Kadapa, Andra Pradesh, India  
⁴ KSRM College of Engineering, Kadap, Andra Pradesh, India  
  

## Abstract

The increasing use of blockchain technology in supply chain management has made it imperative to understand the possible security risks associated with its implementation. This research aims to identify important security issues related to supply chain management's use of blockchain technology by doing a thorough analysis of the body of existing literature and looking at actual cases of blockchain deployments. These dangers include the possibility of data privacy breaches, smart contract weaknesses, and 51% attack vulnerability. The report also offers suggestions for reducing these risks, including using multi-factor authentication, regularly carrying out security audits, and enforcing strict access rules. The study's conclusions broaden our knowledge of the security risks associated with blockchain-based supply chain management (BC-SCM) and offer useful guidance to companies thinking about implementing this technology.

**Keywords:** Blockchain, Data privacy, Information security, Privacy, Supply chain management, Transparency.  
  

* * *

^(\*) **Corresponding author K. Chandrasekaran:** National Institute of Technology Karnataka, Surathkal, Mangalore, India; E-mail: [kch@nitk.edu.in](mailto:kch@nitk.edu.in)  
  

## INTRODUCTION

A key component of corporate operations is supply chain management, and blockchain technology is becoming more and more popular because of its ability to improve supply chains' efficiency, security, and transparency. Blockchain use

in supply chain management has several advantages, including the ability to preserve an immutable transaction record and lower fraud risk, but it also presents serious security risks.

### Blockchain Technology

Blockchain, a decentralized ledger technology, has attracted a lot of interest because of its potential to revolutionize a number of sectors, including finance, healthcare, and logistics management. It basically works as an open, safe system for registering and confirming transactions. Typically, transactions on a blockchain network are thought of as cryptographically connected blocks that, thanks to sophisticated cryptographic methods, form a safe chain. Because blockchain is decentralized, no one party can have total control over the network, which makes it challenging to alter the data that is stored in the blocks. As a result, blockchain is already being heralded as a revolutionary technology with a wide range of possible uses, such as smart contracts and digital currency. Notwithstanding blockchain's potential benefits, implementation is challenging due to issues with scalability, security, and interoperability. The blockchain network is made up of numerous cooperating computers. There are thousands of exact copies of the blockchain since each computer, or ”blockchain node”, retains a copy of it. Because blockchain replication occurs automatically and a chain is built by hashing codes across blocks, it is difficult for an attacker to change the contents in any block. If one block is changed, the entire chain must also be changed, as well as at least 51% of the nodes’ copies of the blockchain, which is not feasible when dealing with a big blockchain or its network.

A block consists of a header, a list of transactions, and a hash, as illustrated in Fig. ([**1**](#9789815305210-C6.html#f1)). The header of a block contains essential information such as the block number, the date it was appended to the chain, and a distinct hash code generated through cryptographic means. This hash code serves to guarantee the integrity and unalterability of the block's data by acting as a cryptographic fingerprint. Additionally, the header includes a link to the preceding block's hash in the chain, facilitating the connection between blocks and the formation of a blockchain. A block's list of transactions includes details about the sender, recipient, the quantity of crypto currency, or other data being transferred. Once a transaction is confirmed, it is appended to the block's list of transactions. A network of nodes within the blockchain network frequently verifies the transactions. The inclusion of a block nonce typically simplifies the verification process, though it may necessitate the use of intricate algorithms and consensus mechanisms. Through a cryptographic hash function, any alteration, even a minor change of a single bit in the block contents, results in an unexpected and minute modification in the hash code.

![](Images/9789815305210-C6-F1.jpg)**Fig. (1))**  
Basic structure of a block in a blockchain. 

In essence, the blockchain's block structure comprises a roster of validated transactions and a block header containing metadata and a link to the preceding block. This arrangement ensures the security, transparency, and immutability of the data stored in the blockchain.

### Smart Contracts

Self-executing contracts, known as “smart contracts”, are stored on a blockchain, a distributed ledger technology that facilitates safe, open transactions without the need for middlemen. Smart contracts are becoming more and more popular because of their ability to automate various company processes, do away with the need for middlemen, and increase operational performance. In addition, smart contracts provide an irreversible, transparent, and secure degree of integrity and trust that is unmatched by traditional contracts. Smart contracts have garnered substantial interest from scholars and practitioners in a range of industries, such as finance, insurance, healthcare, and logistics, in the past several years. When the conditions agreed upon by both parties are met, a smart contract can be thought of as a piece of self-executing code that only executes a predetermined set of instructions. This guarantees that after the contract is made, neither party may back out.

### Supply Chain Management Systems

A supply chain is a well-planned and structured network that links a business to its suppliers in order to produce and market a particular product to consumers in an effort to save costs and preserve market competitiveness. It is composed of multiple entities, processes, data and information flows, and other resources. To put it simply, the supply chain is the series of actions and individuals that move a product from its point of origin to the end user. The initial steps in this process include supplying the raw materials, having them manufactured, distributing them around the market, and providing them to the client. Supply chain operations commonly involve five key participants, namely manufacturers, distributors, retailers, suppliers, and customers. Every entity in the supply chain supplies commodities to others in exchange for the terms and payments that have been agreed upon. Modern supply-chain management solutions leverage blockchain technology to streamline these agreements, payments, and delivery. Achieving cost savings, a fast production cycle, and simplified supply chain processes all depend on effective management. Supply chain management encompasses a wide range of distinct disciplines and operations. There are a variety of supply chain management models with varying levels of functionality on the market.

### STRIDE Framework

Software developers frequently utilize the STRIDE framework, a security threat modeling methodology, to find potential security risks and vulnerabilities in software applications. STRIDE is a mnemonic representing Spoofing, Tampering, Repudiation, Disclosure of Information, Elevation of Privilege, and Denial of Service. Spoofing involves an attacker impersonating a user or system to access sensitive information or resources. Tampering is the capability of an attacker to modify data during transit or while at rest.

The capacity of an attacker to retract completed activities, such as requests or transactions, is known as repudiation. Information disclosure is when private information is uninvitedly given to an uninvited party. An attacker's ability to interfere with or prevent access to a system or application is known as denial of service. The ability of an attacker to obtain elevated access privileges to a system or application is known as “elevation of privilege”.

### Blockchain in Supply Chain Management

The Supply Chain Operations Reference (SCOR) model is one of the most effective and extensively used frameworks for managing and optimizing supply chain management. It offers a uniform method for overseeing supply chain operations in many fields and companies. The five main processes are as follows: Plan, Source, Make, Deliver, and Return. Metrics, best practices, and sub-processes inside each step are further broken down to help measure and enhance supply chain performance. In order to gauge and enhance the supply chain's performance, it also features a set of key performance indicators (KPIs). It is difficult to incorporate every step of the supply chain into a single blockchain since there are so many different procedures and indications involved. However, even a small amount of blockchain integration in the supply chain improves the procedure \[[1](#9789815305210-C6.html#r1), [2](#9789815305210-C6.html#r2)\]. In a supply chain, the manufacturer receives the customer demand pool from retailers and distributors, which is utilized to place supplier orders for raw materials or halt production. The stakeholders benefit from this whole feedback cycle by ensuring that supply and demand are balanced for maximum profit. Within the blockchain framework, authorized individuals can access a decentralized ledger for each party, which ensures internal communication \[[3](#9789815305210-C6.html#r3)^(\-)[5](#9789815305210-C6.html#r5)\]. We can monitor, track, and report items across the supply chain (SC) with the assistance of each partner adding item transactions to the ledger. Parties may additionally engage in the creation of smart contracts. The three fundamental parts of every smart contract are the ledger, the logic, and the properties (both changeable and static). These elements can all be simply translated into technological ideas. A database is represented by the ledger, logic is represented by properties, and data schemas are represented by logic. Here, the payment transactions ledger (*i.e.*, the payment is made) might be the ledger, and the logic could be the delivery of goods (*i.e.*, the tracked product reaches the needed party) \[[6](#9789815305210-C6.html#r6)\]. A P2P file-sharing protocol can be used to store and retrieve data, and a querying protocol can be used to query all of the ledgers, as referred in Fig. ([**2**](#9789815305210-C6.html#f2)).

### Contributions

In this research, we employ the STRIDE framework to evaluate the security vulnerabilities linked to Blockchain-based supply chain management systems. This examination aims to aid security professionals and software developers in recognizing potential threats to the security of software applications.

## LITERATURE REVIEW

This literature review employs the STRIDE framework to provide a comprehensive overview of the current state of research concerning threat modeling in blockchain systems. Previous works have already addressed the application of STRIDE analysis, as outlined in “Secure blockchain in the enterprise: A methodology” \[[31](#9789815305210-C6.html#r31)\] by Cédric Hébert and Francesco Di Cerbo, which outlines steps for completing STRIDE analysis. In their study, they have identified 67 specific threats associated with blockchain technology. Another relevant work on this subject is “On the Applicability of Security and Privacy Threat Modelling for Blockchain Applications” \[32\], which explores areas where elicitative threat modeling techniques can be enhanced. The subsequent sections highlight various types of threats in the context of blockchain.

![](Images/9789815305210-C6-F2.jpg)**Fig. (2))**  
Overview of blockchain-based supply chain manage ment. 

### Blockchain Threat Types

Although blockchain technology can completely transform various businesses, it poses severe privacy and security risks. Blockchain offers a transparent, decentralized ledger that is hard to tamper with but is not immune to attacks, which is a significant problem. For example, assaults can occur when one party controls most of the network's computational capacity, which allows them to potentially manipulate transactions or spend money twice. In a similar vein, attackers may take advantage of smart contracts’ flaws, which automate transactions on the blockchain. Furthermore, even while blockchain enables pseudonymous transactions associated with a specific address rather than a particular person’s identity, this does not ensure total anonymity because these transactions can be tracked and examined. So, to reduce these risks and guarantee the integrity and privacy of the blockchain, it is essential for blockchain developers and users to put security measures in place such as multi-factor authentication, encryption, and thorough auditing of smart contracts. The following sections will look into different security and privacy threat types.

#### Security Threat Types

##### Double Spending

Double spending occurs when a user attempts to spend the same digital currency unit more than once since transactions involving digital currencies are frequently irreversible after they are registered on the blockchain. Otherwise, an attacker can effectively create counterfeit money and profit from it if they are able to spend double. It mostly happens when multiple miners compete to be the first to add a new block to the blockchain during block races. The main culprit is consensus protocol mining, which presents risks like resource waste, participant disadvantage, dishonest profit, and meddling \[[9](#9789815305210-C6.html#r9), [10](#9789815305210-C6.html#r10)\].

##### Mining and Mining Pools

Mining and mining pools pose significant security threats even though they are vital to the blockchain ecosystem. The biggest security risk associated with mining and mining pools is the potential for a 51% attack. If a single miner or mining pool controls more than half of the network's processing power, they could be able to halt the creation of new blocks for the blockchain, change transactions, or spend money twice. Additional security issues include centralization, which can undermine the decentralized nature of blockchain technology, Sybil attacks, which occur when an attacker creates many nodes on the network to control excessive mining power, and mining malware that infects a miner's computer or mining equipment. It is implemented during mining strategy deviations, block races, and financial incentives. The primary problems are incentive incompatibility and consensus protocol mining, which can result in dangers like double spending \[[11](#9789815305210-C6.html#r11)\], disproportionate gains \[[12](#9789815305210-C6.html#r12)\], resource waste \[[13](#9789815305210-C6.html#r13)\], and 51% attack \[[14](#9789815305210-C6.html#r14)\].

##### Peer-to-peer (P2P)

Peer-to-peer (P2P) networks are an essential component of blockchain technology because they let users communicate and deal with each other directly, bypassing intermediaries. They also present security flaws that need to be fixed to maintain the secrecy and integrity of the blockchain. One of the biggest security dangers is the potential for a Sybil attack, in which a hacker fabricates numerous false identities or network nodes in order to control an excessive amount of power. It takes place when multiple nodes are deployed, peers are identified, and connections are made. The root culprit is the decentralized Internet overlay, which increases the possibility of self-serving mining, double spending, denial of service, and node isolation \[[15](#9789815305210-C6.html#r15)\].

#### Privacy Threat Types

##### Blockchain Analysis

Since the blockchain is a public ledger, everyone with network access can see every transaction. This makes it possible for people to examine and track transactions to ascertain the parties involved, the history of their activities, and the total amount of cryptocurrency exchanged. Even legal transactions may reveal private information about the parties involved. It is possible to use this information to identify individuals or organizations involved in criminal activities. Blockchain analysis tools enable the correlation of transactions with specific people or entities, the tracking of money transfers across the blockchain, and the identification of behavioral patterns. Although blockchain research has the potential to prevent fraud and other illegal activities, it also puts users' privacy in danger. It operates during the following processes: active transaction execution, address and user network, clustering heuristics, combining with off-chain data, and behavior-based clustering. Transparency, public nature, and pseudonymity are the root causes that rise to concerns like participant identification and transaction linking \[[16](#9789815305210-C6.html#r16)^(\-)[20](#9789815305210-C6.html#r20)\].

##### Network Analysis

A method known as network analysis can be used to examine network traffic in order to identify behavioral patterns and identify the nodes or companies that were a part of the connection. In the context of blockchain, network analysis can be used to monitor the information flow between nodes and potentially uncover the identities of the parties involved in transactions. This information can be used to ascertain the amount of bitcoin that each participant exchanged as well as their transaction history. Network analysis can be used to locate nodes and determine their IP addresses, which can be used to determine participant identities. It takes effect during TOR attacks *via* DoS defense, anomalies in relay patterns, and the propagation of IP addresses and transactions. P2P networks are the root problem, and they pose threats like linking transactions to IP addresses \[[21](#9789815305210-C6.html#r21)^(\-)[23](#9789815305210-C6.html#r23)\].

##### Key Management

Network traffic can be analyzed using a technique called network analysis to spot patterns of behavior and pinpoint the nodes or organizations that were involved in the connection. Network analysis can be used in the context of blockchain to track the information flow between nodes and perhaps reveal the identities of the parties taking part in transactions. The quantity of bitcoin exchanged by the participants, as well as their transaction history, may be determined using this data. The location and IP addresses of nodes can be found through network analysis, which may be used to identify participants. It operates during Bloom filters in SPV and the extraction of private keys. The underlying issue lies in cryptographic foundations, resulting in potential risks like linking transactions to one another and associating them with IP addresses \[[24](#9789815305210-C6.html#r24)^(\-)[26](#9789815305210-C6.html#r26)\].

## GAP ANALYSIS

Supply chain management based on blockchain is gradually being adopted; thus, it is important to identify any security risks associated with the system. In order to identify potential risks in blockchain-based supply chain management systems, this paper applies the STRIDE methodology. Although the use of blockchain in supply chain management is discussed extensively in publications, new dangers are not given enough attention. This document also addresses tools and implementation guidelines for the STRIDE framework to assist organizations in implementing the suggestions. A lack of systematic threat modeling strategy can result in the implementation of insufficient security solutions, leading to cyberattacks and financial loss. This paper sheds light on ways of implementing structured threat modeling using the STRIDE framework to tackle the above problem.

## DISCUSSION

To identify potential security threats in a system, we must first understand the architecture of the system and the interaction mechanisms of users with the system. Fig. ([**3**](#9789815305210-C6.html#f3)) (Lodovica, Michele, Robert, Maria (2022) \[[7](#9789815305210-C6.html#r7)\] displays the architecture of a typical blockchain setup in an industrial environment. In this architecture model, there are several endpoints and levels in the blockchain ecosystem.

Poston’s step-by-step guide for performing threat modeling on a blockchain system using the STRIDE framework includes the following:

*•* Define the system and its components.

*•* Identify the assets that the system is designed to protect.

*•* Identify the potential threats to the system using the STRIDE framework.

*•* Evaluate the likelihood and potential impact of each threat.

*•* Develop mitigation strategies to address the most significant threats.

*•* Test and validate the effectiveness of the mitigation strategies.

![](Images/9789815305210-C6-F3.jpg)**Fig. (3))**  
Architecture of a blockchain for industrial applications (Lodovica, Michele, Robert, Maria (2022) \[[7](#9789815305210-C6.html#r7)\]. 

To perform threat analysis using the STRIDE framework, we will divide the blockchain into multiple levels. At each level, we identify threats that involve Spoofing, Tampering, Repudiation, Information Disclosure, Denial of Service, and Elevated Privileges (STRIDE). We can divide the blockchain ecosystem into multiple levels:

1) Fundamental Level

2) Protocol Level

3) Infrastructure Level

4) Advanced Level

### Fundamental Level

Blockchain technology is predicated on block and chain data structures as well as cryptographic primitives. The mathematical algorithms that are employed to safeguard the blockchain system are known as cryptographic primitives. Hashing, digital signatures, and encryption are some examples of these algorithms. Data is protected both at rest and in transit with encryption. Data authenticity and tampering prevention are achieved through the use of digital signatures. Data integrity can be confirmed by using the distinct digital fingerprint that hashing creates. The fundamental building blocks of the blockchain system are the block and chain data structures. A collection of confirmed and recorded transactions may be found in every block of the chain. In addition, every block has a distinct cryptographic hash that is computed using the block's contents. This hash ensures that the contents in the block cannot be changed without being noticed by acting as a digital fingerprint for the block.

#### Cryptographic Primitives

Various cryptographic protocols and systems are built using fundamental mathematical techniques known as cryptographic primitives. These primitives are crucial for ensuring modern communication systems’ security because they are made to give data with secrecy, integrity, and authentication. In a blockchain, we use various cryptographic primitives including encryption, hash functions, digital signatures, and key agreement protocols \[[8](#9789815305210-C6.html#r8)\]. The most commonly used is hash function in public key cryptography, which ensures access control and data integrity in the chain.

#### Data Structures

In the blockchain, transactions are organized into blocks that are interconnected to create a chain-like structure. Each block contains a set of authenticated transactions validated by the network, along with a header containing metadata such as a timestamp and a reference to the previous block. This design ensures that each block is securely linked to every other block, forming an indelible and interconnected chain of blocks. On the other hand, flaws in the data structures or the way they are handled could affect the blockchain's security. For instance, if the transaction data format is poorly specified, attackers may be able to change it to carry out fraudulent operations, including making fictitious transactions or double-spending. Similarly, attackers can alter the contents of a block or even add new blocks to the chain by manipulating the header if it is not securely hashed.

The structure of data within a blockchain plays a crucial role in determining the security and susceptibility of the system. The data structure of a blockchain is responsible for organizing and securing the information on the network, encompassing details about transactions, blocks, and the overall state of the system. Weaknesses in the system, resulting from an inadequately designed data structure, could be exploited by attackers. One potential source of double-spending attacks is a data structure that allows for the creation of orphaned blocks. Orphaned blocks are those that are not part of the main chain and are therefore disregarded when determining the state of the blockchain. An attacker can use a series of controlled orphaned blocks to commit double-spending.

Another instance of how a data structure might affect a blockchain's security is the 2016 DAO attack on the Ethereum network. A decentralized autonomous organization called the DAO was constructed on top of the Ethereum network. Voting on investment decisions was intended to be possible with the DAO, with results being tracked on the blockchain. However, an attacker was able to take advantage of a reentrancy vulnerability due to a fault in the DAO's smart contract, which allowed them to steal Ethereum valued at millions of dollars.

In addition to vulnerabilities, the data structure of a blockchain can also impact the scalability and performance of the system. For example, a data structure that is inefficient for storing and retrieving large amounts of data can result in slower transaction times and reduced overall performance.

### Protocol Level

The set of guidelines and practices known as blockchain protocols makes it possible for a distributed, decentralized ledger system to operate. The block formation process and consensus algorithms are two crucial elements of these protocols that support network security and integrity.

#### Consensus

The techniques that allow nodes in the network to concur on the ledger's current state are known as consensus algorithms. A decentralized system lacks a central authority to keep track of transactions or judge their legitimacy. Rather, nodes need to validate and verify the ledger in order to reach a consensus on its current status. Attackers can use the network to carry out malicious operations, such as forging fraudulent transactions or seizing control of them if the consensus process is inadequate.

#### Block Creation

The method by which new blocks are appended to the blockchain is called the block creation process. To validate transactions and add new blocks, nodes in a decentralized network must cooperate with one another. The following steps are usually involved in the process:

1) Users broadcast transactions to the network.

2) The network's nodes validate and verify transactions to make sure they are real and haven't been double-spent.

3) A block is created from all of the validated transactions.

4) Consensus techniques are used to validate blocks.

5) The blockchain is updated with validated blocks.

The decentralized and safe maintenance of the ledger is facilitated by the block creation process. Since several nodes in the network validate each block, it is challenging for one node to tamper with the ledger. Furthermore, the network's resistance to attacks and tampering is ensured by the use of cryptography and consensus procedures.

### Infrastructure Level

Blockchain infrastructure refers to the underlying technological framework that enables the functioning of a blockchain network. It is a decentralized and distributed infrastructure that facilitates the creation, storage, and exchange of data and value across a network of computers.

#### Nodes

The endpoints, or nodes or devices that run the blockchain software, are the central components of the blockchain infrastructure. These endpoints are in charge of handling transactions, verifying blocks, and preserving the blockchain's integrity. The blockchain ledger is replicated at every endpoint and is updated and synchronized in real-time throughout the network.

#### Network

The network that links the endpoints is another component of the blockchain infrastructure. The nature of the blockchain network determines whether it is public or private. Whereas participation in a private blockchain network is limited to a select set of authorized endpoints, anybody can join a public blockchain network and become an endpoint.

### Advanced Level

Without the requirement for a central authority, peer-to-peer transactions may be conducted securely and effectively using the fundamental blockchain technology. However, the development of smart contract platforms and APIs has expanded the capabilities of blockchain technology.

#### Smart Contracts

Smart contracts are self-executing contracts that are created by instantly incorporating the conditions of the agreement between the seller and the buyer into lines of code. Smart contracts are designed to automate the execution of contracts without requiring a central authority. This enables the creation of complex transactions that may be completed in a transparent and safe manner.

#### APIs

Blockchain technology has been extended through the use of Application Programming Interfaces (APIs). APIs allow external software and devices to interact with the blockchain network, providing additional functionality and connectivity. This integration can provide businesses with the ability to automate processes and access blockchain data without needing to create their own blockchain infrastructure.

## RESEARCH METHODOLOGY

For this study, we employed a Systematic Literature Review (SLR) methodology. The results of the SLR have been synthesized using the narrative synthesis framework. This method was used because the extant literature's study designs were heterogeneous, and there was not enough qualitative data to conduct a meta-analysis. The threat modeling for blockchains put forth by Howard Poston in his blog article served as the foundation for the analytical methodology. The main ideas and themes that guided the literature search, selection, organization, and analysis were outlined in this framework. Following the development of the fundamental analytical framework, a thorough search of the pertinent literature was conducted using keyword-based querying. The search terms and databases selected in the search strategy were applied to reputable journals such as IEEE, ACM, Springer, Elsevier, and ResearchGate. Among the keywords that were used were “vulnerability” AND “blockchain”, “smart contracts” AND “threats”, and “threat analysis” AND “vulnerability”. Finding papers that highlighted weaknesses in infrastructure, smart contracts, protocols, and API endpoints pertinent to a supply chain management system based on blockchain technology was the inclusion criterion that was adhered to. The investigation did not include any literature on the financial risks associated with utilizing supply chain management systems based on blockchain technology. Additionally, we have gathered and examined data from numerous blog postings and peer-reviewed journals. Every piece of information and discovery was arranged to conform to the analytical framework, as depicted in Table **1**.
