# Automated Production Management in Horticulture: An Industry 4.0 Perspective

  
  
**Archna^([1](#9789815305210-C12.html#aff1), [\*](#9789815305210-C12.html#cor1))****,** **Gursharan Singh^([2](#9789815305210-C12.html#aff2))****,** **Nidhi Bhagat^([1](#9789815305210-C12.html#aff1))****,** **Sakshi Thakur^([1](#9789815305210-C12.html#aff1))**  
¹ Mittal School of Business, Lovely Professional University, Phagwara, India  
² School of Allied Medical Sciences, Lovely Professional University, Phagwara, India  
  

## Abstract

The whole industry has changed as it progresses from 1.0 to 4.0 in accordance with regulations. Horticulture has seen tremendous advances in automated technology production management, which has revolutionized crop cultivation and management techniques. These technological developments have improved product quality, increased crop productivity, decreased labor costs, and reduced negative environmental consequences. The purpose of this book chapter is to investigate the current state of automated production management in horticulture, including its applications, techniques, and potential future directions. This chapter presents an overview of how technology is being used in automated production management, such as robotic harvesting, sensor-based monitoring, and precision irrigation. In addition, the chapter investigates the possible benefits and future possibilities of automated production management in horticulture. It focuses on developing trends, such as the incorporation of artificial intelligence and machine learning algorithms, which can improve decision-making processes and resource allocation. Data analytics, remote sensing, and Internet of Things (IoT) technologies are also covered for real-time monitoring and system optimization. Some aspects of automated horticulture are also investigated, such as crop diversity, development phases, and environmental variables.

Finally, this book chapter presents a complete review of automated production management in horticulture, emphasizing the industry's disruptive significance. It also tackles farmers' and researchers' existing issues with automated horticulture production systems. Horticulture stakeholders may improve productivity, sustainability, and profitability by embracing automated production management in an increasingly competitive and resource-constrained environment.

**Keywords:** Controlled environment agriculture, Integrated pest harvesting, Precision irrigation, Post harvesting, Remote monitoring, Robotic-crop care.  
  

* * *

^(\*) **Corresponding author Archna:** Mittal School of Business, Lovely Professional University, Phagwara, India; E-mail: [makkerarchana@gmail.com](mailto:makkerarchana@gmail.com)  
  

## THE ORETICAL BACKGROUND

It is anticipated that there will be nine billion people in the world by 2030. The majority of population increase will occur in developing countries in Asia, Africa, and Latin America, where hunger and food scarcity are issues. Horticulture is underdeveloped in the majority of African countries. The region's food priority may be addressed by focusing attention on key horticulture crops and critical challenges. Food shortages, distribution, nutrition, a country's or region's competitive edge, constraints on local and export marketing, and industry strategic planning, including institutional, regulatory, and technological improvements, are some of the primary challenges. This paper discusses the constraints and benefits of combining nutrition education with agricultural research aims, as well as the huge potential of horticulture crops in addressing food scarcity and malnutrition in Africa \[[1](#9789815305210-C12.html#r1)\]. The article describes a less costly and labor-intensive method of clonal propagation that uses modified air-lift, bubble column, bioreactors (a balloon-type bubble bioreactor), and temporary immersion systems for the propagation of shoots, bud clusters, and somatic embryos. It explains how to grow Anoectochilus, apple, ginseng, garlic, grape, Lilium, Phalaenopsis, and potatoes. This chapter addresses bioreactor features and the design of bioreactor procedures for automated mass multiplication of diverse plant crops. It also highlights new research aimed at increasing the automation of the bioreactor manufacturing process \[[2](#9789815305210-C12.html#r2)\]. Growers are having a difficult time right now because they are under pressure to improve the quality of their products owing to worldwide competition. New safety and procedural regulations must be followed all the way up the food chain. Everyone should prioritize reducing the negative environmental consequences of the industrial cycle in all aspects of life. In general, agriculture has been urged to reduce production costs in order to compete and live on government aid. Almost everywhere in the globe, there are suppliers for the processing industries. New players are eager to emerge and take on crucial responsibilities all across the world. Fresh food distribution has benefited from technology in previously unimaginable ways \[[3](#9789815305210-C12.html#r3)\]. The horticulture sector has historically transformed the landscape of the United States through planned cultivar introductions as well as unintended introductions of weeds, insects, and plant diseases. The sales of established invasive plants persist, and the number of new cultivars introduced annually is rising despite the fact that the horticultural sector—especially, the ornamental subsector—has been demonstrated to play a major role in the introduction and spread of invasive species. This study characterizes the distribution channel's complexity and investigates the horticulture trade as a vector for invasive species and associated agents. The recent increase in commercialized cultivars has been linked to a variety of causes, including technological breakthroughs, industrial development, and marketing initiatives. As a result, consumer demand has increased and become more sophisticated, resulting in an intense hunt for novel crops across the world. Many of these crops are introduced into the market without being fully assessed for invasive potential. Traditional techniques for managing invasive horticulture crops (regulation, self-regulation) have had limited effectiveness and buy-in because they overlook the industry's intricacies and financial motivations. These methods concentrate on distributors both before and after crop release \[[4](#9789815305210-C12.html#r4)\]. The study examined the productivity of agricultural crops under the conventional agri-horticulture system, as well as the structure, composition, and diversity of fruit tree and shrub species in the mid-hill region of the Garhwal Himalaya, India, between 1000 and 2000 m asl during the summer and winter seasons on the northern and southern aspects. Depending on the aspect, landholding, and farmer requests, the system's tree density, composition, and diversity varied. Twelve fruit tree species were identified in the agri-horticulture system; four of these were found to be common in both the northern and southern aspects, while the other six were found to be common exclusively in the northern aspect and the other two in the southern. It was found that the apple tree (Malus domestica) had the greatest IVI values on both the northern and southern sides \[[5](#9789815305210-C12.html#r5)\]. Horticultural crops, with high yields and export value, play an important part in world nutrition. However, the sector is confronted with issues like resource shortages, global warming, and the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic. To solve these concerns, smart farming, sustainable agriculture, and precision farming are becoming more popular. Digitization, robotics, automation, artificial intelligence, and IoT are key to attaining sustainable production and solving obstacles ranging from farming to marketing. The review emphasizes the importance of transitioning to high-tech horticulture to ensure productivity and economic stability in the face of changing climate, growing population, and consumption patterns, advocating for machinery and automation to meet global demand. When compared to typical fluorescent lighting, LED treatments consistently resulted in better plant biomass, fruit output, and energy usage efficiency. Notably, LED illumination increased the antioxidant content of basil leaves while decreasing the nitrate concentration. The study discovered that a red:blue ratio of 0.7 is required for good plant growth and better nutraceutical qualities in both leafy and fruit vegetable crops. Overall, the data show that LED technology has the potential to improve production and nutritional quality in indoor farming \[[6](#9789815305210-C12.html#r6)\]. This analysis looks at the historical backdrop and future implications of the industrial and agricultural revolutions, with an emphasis on the difficulties and opportunities of adopting Industry 4.0 in agriculture. While Industry 4.0 has grown quickly in the industry, debates regarding Industry 5.0 have already begun, in contrast to agriculture adoption's sluggish growth. The study emphasizes the disparity in influence between big firms and small- to medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in both sectors, stressing the challenges that SMEs confront in keeping up with fast technological progress. The report closes by recommending policymakers develop policies and solicit recommendations to assist SMEs in integrating Industry 4.0 technology, thus increasing their competitiveness in a shifting economy \[[7](#9789815305210-C12.html#r7)\]. This project investigates the integration of automation and robotics across all phases of agriculture, with a particular emphasis on the effective watering of horticultural areas, parks, gardens, and golf courses to reduce water waste. Through the careful selection of chemicals, the installation of automation and smart systems helps food safety. Furthermore, multitasking robots help to improve pace while retaining quality. Smart agricultural procedures, made possible by sensors and applications, aid in the regulation of humidity, temperature, and irrigation operations. The major goal of this research is to discover and define the potential for automation and the Internet of Things (IoT) in the agriculture business \[[8](#9789815305210-C12.html#r8)\]. The paper dives into the ubiquitous impact of Industry 4.0 on industrial businesses, a topic that has been mostly studied from a technological standpoint in the current literature. To fill this void, the study takes a three-pronged strategy that includes a literature review, structured interviews, and a synthesis of existing information. The emphasis is on examining the impact of Industry 4.0 on employees, with a particular emphasis on the horticulture industry in Australia—a sector that has received little attention in previous studies. The study reveals extensive evidence of the major effects of Industry 4.0 on employees and employment, both at the macro and micro levels of the labor market, using Scopus, Web of Science, and targeted keywords. The findings help to establish and validate a comprehensive research Programme by clarifying the numerous effects of Industry 4.0 on personnel. Overall, the findings highlight the far-reaching changes occurring in numerous dimensions of human labor as a result of Industry 4.0, emphasizing the need for more investigation and understanding in this changing context \[[9](#9789815305210-C12.html#r9)\]. This study uses a literature review and computer-based qualitative data analysis, as well as Leximancer text mining tools, to uncover major concepts and themes in horticultural developments during the last decade. The software generates an interactive concept map that highlights interconnected themes such as “water,” “urban,” “system,” “countries,” and “technology.” The analysis emphasizes the researcher's interpretive role in deriving meaning from the content while acknowledging the software's data coding support. The study focuses on the dissemination of innovation and obstacles, emphasizing the differences between developing and industrialized nations. This study's comprehensive knowledge provides ideas for future research in the realm of horticulture advances \[[10](#9789815305210-C12.html#r10)\]. The United Nations has made combating global hunger and malnutrition a priority, with an emphasis on the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs 2 and 12). This research is the first to highlight the revolutionary importance of Industry 4.0 technologies in horticulture, such as the Internet of Things, cloud computing, artificial intelligence, blockchain, and big data. It emphasizes the use of modern technologies in conventional activities such as disease detection, irrigation and fertilizer management, maturity determination, marketing, supply chain management, soil fertility, and weather patterns. The study outlines obstacles and makes critical recommendations for future work, emphasising the importance of robotics, vision-enabled drones, and artificial intelligence in identifying pests, infections, and malnutrition. Furthermore, it argues for the development of edge-computing portable devices that use IoT and AI to detect crop disease, providing critical improvements for sustainable and technologically enhanced horticulture \[[11](#9789815305210-C12.html#r11)\]. The Internet of Things (IoT) and cyber-physical systems (CPS) are revolutionary features of Industry 4.0, which signals a considerable divergence from traditional industrial automation. Cognitive automation is being driven by these technologies, ushering in an era of intelligent manufacturing and the development of smart goods and services. However, as businesses negotiate a more dynamic environment, this move presents obstacles. Notably, a quantity of data does not always imply an increase in productivity. This book chapter goes into the ideas of IoT and machine learning, looking at how they might be applied within the context of Industry 4.0. The chapter covers a wide range of topics, such as IoT solutions, edge computing, agriculture, smart cities, healthcare, and military operations \[[12](#9789815305210-C12.html#r12)\]. This analysis digs at the main concerns confronting future food sustainability, such as climate change, population expansion, and food waste, while emphasizing Industry 4.0's revolutionary significance. Part II of the paper explores emerging trends such as 3D printed foods, as well as a comprehensive overview of key technologies within this industrial revolution, such as artificial intelligence, big data analytics, the Internet of Things, blockchain, smart sensors, robotics, digital twins, and cyber-physical systems. The incorporation of these technologies has had a huge influence on the food business, affecting environmental, economic, and health issues. The Food Industry 4.0 era ushers in new challenges, opportunities, and trends, changing tactics and preparing the path for the industry 5.0 transition \[[13](#9789815305210-C12.html#r13)\]. Agriculture 4.0, the fourth agricultural revolution, aims to improve farming through digital technology, promoting precision agriculture through data-driven resource allocation strategies. In contemporary farming, the integration of novel ecosystems emphasizes implementation, integration, and networking. Precision agriculture is a technological solution that employs Big Data technology to improve quality and production by using enormous amounts of data collected in supply chain activities. This chapter presents an overview of Industry 4.0, emphasizing technological interventions and digital changes in post-harvesting processes, with smart farming serving as the conclusion of these advancements. Agriculture 4.0 incorporates digitally enabled agricultural machinery that communicates, assesses processing conditions, determines material needs, and effectively coordinates operations \[[14](#9789815305210-C12.html#r14)\]. Global labor disruptions are threatening the agriculture sector as a result of shifting geopolitics, demography, and the influence of COVID-19.

In the United Kingdom, difficulties connected to free labor mobility and safe working conditions, exacerbated by Brexit and the pandemic, have resulted in a scarcity of agricultural workers. To solve this, the employment of autonomous robots in labor-intensive industries such as soft fruit cultivation is being investigated. While robots for illness treatment and logistical assistance are already in use, those for picking are still in the testing stage. Grower opinions towards autonomous robots on soft fruit farms are positive, according to a mixed-methods study conducted in the United Kingdom, yet variables such as cost, infrastructure, data ownership, and trust pose barriers to deployment. Globally relevant recommendations for industry and policy are provided \[[15](#9789815305210-C12.html#r15)\]. Establishing a professional network to facilitate the successful implementation of innovations in horticultural value chains is the goal of the Horticulture 4.0 project, which was introduced as part of the German finance priority Horticulture 4.0. Announced in 2020, its main goal is to link several research and development (R&D) initiatives related to Horticulture 4.0. The project analyses how digital transformation impacts the horticultural industry's competitiveness, sustainability, and structure through meta-level technical and economic evaluations. Innovation clusters, including technology development, digital processes, information systems, and technology assessment, were formed to streamline R&D operations. Communicating R&D accomplishments to stakeholders and professionals aims to guarantee wide dissemination while addressing innovations and concerns that are significant to society and politics for the broader public \[[16](#9789815305210-C12.html#r16)\].

## RESEARCH METHODOLOGY

This book chapter on automated production management in horticulture employs a complete and balanced research strategy that incorporates qualitative methodologies. To lay a firm basis, an extensive literature study is undertaken to discover current trends, developments, and research needs in the subject. The descriptive study approach used allows for an in-depth investigation of the present status of automated production management. Secondary data is gathered by doing literature research in the horticulture and technology areas. The research focuses on the integration of artificial intelligence and machine learning algorithms, emphasizing their ability to optimize decision-making processes and resource allocation. Furthermore, the study looks at the factors and obstacles involved with automated horticulture, such as high initial investment prices and technological complexity. The overarching purpose of this study is to give a detailed knowledge of the transformational influence of automated production management in horticulture, providing significant insights into productivity and sustainability, as well as recommendations for future research endeavors.

## INTRODUCTION

Production in the horticulture sector refers to the process of cultivating, developing, and harvesting plants for a variety of reasons, including medical applications, food, decorative usage, and landscaping. It encompasses a wide range of tasks associated with the cultivation and management of plants, including fruits, vegetables, flowers, herbs, and nursery crops. The horticultural production process consists of several stages and duties, beginning with crop planning and propagation and ending with field preparation. Planting, crop maintenance, harvesting, and post-harvest management follow.

These sessions include important topics including nursery management and field preparation. Young plants are fostered until they are ready for transplantation under the activity of nursery management, while field preparation includes soil preparation and site removal. Planting follows, with young plants carefully carried from nurseries to the field or greenhouse, assuring suitable procedures and spacing. Crop care is an important component that includes techniques such as irrigation, fertilization, pest and disease management, pruning, and weed control, all of which contribute to healthy plant development and yield maximization. Harvesting marks are crucial milestones because mature plants or plant parts are carefully picked when they have reached their ideal developmental stage for consumption or commercial usage. To preserve quality and extend shelf life, the harvested food is subsequently subjected to post-harvest management operations such as sorting, grading, packing, and proper storage. Horticulture production may also include value-added processing, which converts horticulture crops into processed commodities such as jams, preserves, dried herbs, or flower arrangements, adding value and broadening product options. Horticultural production methods can range from typical open fields to protected habitats such as greenhouses, vertical farming systems, and hydroponic setups. The technique used is determined by elements such as the individual crop, geographical climate, market needs, and production goals. The primary objective of horticulture production is to maximize yield, quality, and profitability while achieving efficient and sustainable crop development. Plant biology, cultivation practices, pest and disease control, soil and water management, and market dynamics are all required. Horticulture production adds to the availability of fresh produce, improves visual appeal, promotes environmental sustainability, and drives economic growth within the horticulture industry by employing proper methods.

### Meaning of Automated

Hand weed control is one of the final manuals for labor-intensive agricultural operations that can be mechanized with autonomous robotic weed control devices. Furthermore, robotic technology has the potential to reduce agriculture's present dependency on pesticides, boost sustainability, and reduce environmental effects. The present status of four crucial technologies—mapping, guiding, detection and identification, and precision in-row weed removal—that are necessary for the successful design of a general-purpose robotic weed management system is examined in this research. The most difficult of the four tasks is still identifying and detecting weeds in the wide range of environmental conditions typical in agricultural regions. The potential of the technology has been proved by a few fully functional robotic weed control systems \[[17](#9789815305210-C12.html#r17)\]. Precision agriculture is a set of technologies that combines sensors, information systems, improved equipment, and informed management to maximize production while accounting for the unpredictability and uncertainties of agricultural systems. Optimizing production inputs for each animal and each site within a field allows for more effective resource usage, preserving environmental quality and increasing food supply sustainability. Precision agriculture allows for greater control over the quantity and quality of agricultural goods produced while keeping an eye on the food supply chain \[[18](#9789815305210-C12.html#r18)\]. To improve the usage of water for agricultural purposes an automated irrigation system was developed. A scattered wireless system related to the network for temperature, moisture, and soil is included in the automated irrigation. Sensors are placed in the root zone of the plant. A gateway device also organizes sensor data, initiates actuators, and delivers data to an internet application. An algorithm containing temperature and soil moisture threshold values was designed and encoded into a microcontroller-based gateway to restrict the amount of water utilized. The system was powered by solar energy and included a duplex communication link based on a cellular Internet interface that allowed irrigation to be scheduled and data to be inspected *via* a web page \[[19](#9789815305210-C12.html#r19)\]. The growing global population is driving a transition towards increasingly advanced farming methods. Most nations are very concerned about food security because of the loss of natural resources, the scarcity of arable land, and the increase in unpredictability of weather. Consequently, there is a growing use of data analytics (DA) and the Internet of Things (IoT) to increase agricultural productivity and operational effectiveness. Wireless sensor networks (WSN) are being replaced by IoT and DA as the primary forces underpinning smart agriculture. A number of modern technologies are combined to form the Internet of Things (IoT), such as end-user apps, radio frequency identification, cloud computing, wireless sensor networks (WSN), and middleware systems. This paper outlines the benefits and drawbacks of IoT \[[20](#9789815305210-C12.html#r20)\]. The use of modern technology and systems to automate and simplify different elements of agricultural production is referred to as automation. It entails merging automation, robots, sensing technology, and data analytics to improve horticulture production, efficiency, and resource management. Horticulture has seen tremendous changes over time, with new technologies ushering in significant modifications to old techniques. This study investigates the basic differences between contemporary and traditional horticultural techniques, offering light on their distinct strengths, shortcomings, and productivity and sustainability consequences. Cutting-edge technology, superior breeding techniques, and precision agriculture all play important roles in modern horticulture. GMOs, hybrid seeds, and chemical fertilizers have resulted in higher agricultural yields and disease resistance. Furthermore, controlled environment agriculture, which includes hydroponics, aeroponics, and vertical farming, has optimized resource utilization while minimizing environmental impacts. Traditional horticulture, on the other hand, is steeped in time-honored wisdom, relying on local knowledge and practices passed down through generations. Traditional farmers choose open-field farming, heritage seeds, and organic fertilizers in their pursuit of environmental harmony and sustainability. They work to maintain biodiversity and cultural legacy by following seasonal cycles and cultivating a varied range of local crops. The ramifications of these disparate techniques on crop quality, environmental effects, resource management, and economic feasibility are assessed in this comparative analysis. Modern agricultural practices have fueled agricultural development and production; nonetheless, concerns have been raised about chemical use and the loss of traditional crop diversity. Traditional horticulture, on the other hand, has the potential to develop resilient ecosystems, promote local food systems, and protect cultural heritage.

### Comparison of Traditional and Modern Process

Fig. ([**1**](#9789815305210-C12.html#f1)) shows that Crop selection, propagation, nursery management, field preparation, planting, crop care, harvesting, post-harvest handling, and marketing are all part of the horticulture production system. Monitoring and record-keeping are critical in this sophisticated cycle, allowing informed judgments and adaptive management techniques. Specific techniques may differ depending on crop kind, production volume, and industry norms. Traditional horticulture production systems, on the other hand, are based on long-standing practices and local expertise and rely on open-field cultivation, crop rotation, human labor, natural fertilizers, traditional irrigation, and unique pest and disease management tactics. Traditional horticulture production is built on the adoption of seed-saving procedures, as well as the reliance on accumulated knowledge and expertise passed down through generations. This dual viewpoint emphasizes the growing modern ways alongside the tenacious ancient methods, both of which contribute to the diverse domain of horticulture. While old production methods have their advantages, it is vital to recognize that current agricultural practices and technology have evolved to meet productivity, resource optimization, and sustainability concerns in horticulture. Integrating traditional knowledge with contemporary techniques has the potential to lead to more efficient and sustainable horticulture production systems. Horticulture has seen considerable changes year after year; as time passes, contemporary inventions bring many alterations to conventional techniques. After reviewing horticulture-related papers, this study concludes that there is a fundamental difference between modern and traditional horticulture processes, shedding light on their respective strengths and weaknesses and having an impact on horticulture productivity and sustainability. Agriculture, according to the traditional technique, lays a significant focus on the wisdom passed down through generations and depends on natural pest control and soil fertility measures. It optimizes resource utilization by following seasonal cues and harmonizing with nature. Modern agriculture, on the other hand, makes use of technology, machinery, and genetically modified crops to increase production, address food security problems, and promote sustainability. While contemporary tactics have indisputable benefits, old methods remain critical in protecting local ecosystems, cultural history, and sustainable farming traditions. Integrating the qualities of both systems provides a balanced and holistic answer to the issues of feeding the world's rising population while protecting the environment and varied cultural traditions. Marketing, distribution, sales, and feedback are critical components of contemporary agriculture, contributing to efficient and sustainable methods. Modern farmers can recognize market demands and customer preferences using cutting-edge technologies and data-driven methodologies, allowing them to customize their agricultural production appropriately. Advanced distribution networks allow agricultural goods to travel seamlessly, reducing post-harvest losses and assuring timely delivery to customers. Modern sales strategies also encourage direct connections between suppliers and customers, fostering openness and fair-trade practices. Farmers may make informed decisions, optimize resource utilization, and improve product quality by building continuous feedback loops enabled by digital platforms and precision agriculture instruments, boosting agricultural innovation and growth in a quickly changing environment. So, Modern farming practices perform better than traditional ones by increasing production, sustainability, and efficiency. Improved resource management and increased yields are the outcomes of innovations such as genetically modified crops, precision farming, and sophisticated irrigation systems. These developments address issues like population growth and climate change more successfully than conventional methods.

## TECHNOLOGY APPLICATION IN AUTOMATED PRODUCTION MANAGEMENT

Fig. ([**2**](#9789815305210-C12.html#f2)) relates to technology application in automated production management, which shows that precision agriculture is becoming increasingly crucial as advances in information and communication technology, robotics, and sensor technologies continue. While precise management has several advantages, including environmental friendliness and increased resource efficiency, the initial expenses are a worry. Using partial budgeting as a tool, this study compares the economic feasibility of a precision irrigation and harvest management system based on sensor technologies and a farm management information system (FMIS) to conventional approaches. Precision management is economically viable in situations of high irrigation demand and unpredictable weather conditions, according to the study, which was conducted on an apple orchard in Prangins, Switzerland, but its practicality is dependent on factors such as fruit pricing and capital costs. Agricultural robotics research has considerably revolutionized the sector, particularly in precision livestock farming, by allowing machine skills such as sensing, reasoning, communication, task execution, and systems integration \[[21](#9789815305210-C12.html#r21)\]. While the focus has mostly been on perception and reasoning, there has been little progress in task execution and system integration. Monitors, harvesters, and hybrids are the three types of agricultural robots. Developing specialized robots for specific duties in chicken farming is a notable problem, but novel solutions are emerging. Future studies will likely concentrate on a systems approach to improving efficiency in chicken house management. The essay investigates precision irrigation (PI) in smart urban agriculture, which employs IoT sensors to optimize crop watering. However, it emphasizes PI systems' vulnerability to hackers when data is transferred *via* the Internet. The research advocates merging blockchain technology with PI to preserve IoT sensors and overcome deployment problems to improve security and trust in the agricultural supply chain. It offers insights into attack models, a blockchain integration model, and a case study (Agri Chain), and serves as a guide for stakeholders and researchers aiming to establish a secure and efficient ecosystem for IoT-based PI \[[22](#9789815305210-C12.html#r22)\]. It aims to improve knowledge of IoT business models by investigating design possibilities and alternatives. Morphological analysis is performed through case studies to construct a generic IoT business model, presenting numerous choices for each of the four business model parameters. This study not only identifies similarities and differences among IoT-based business models but also introduces an innovative application of morphological analysis for business model innovation in the context of IoT applications in emerging markets \[[23](#9789815305210-C12.html#r23)\]. The technology adjusts irrigation schedules depending on local vegetation differences during the growing season to maximize agricultural productivity and water efficiency. Six hundred and ninety three control nodes organized into 15 m × 15 m cells are directed by a central computer, allowing for individualized and exact watering. This system demonstrated a remarkable 26% yield increase in the second year and an average 16% water use efficiency improvement after two years of operation, demonstrating the potential of closed-loop automation and advanced irrigation analytics for optimizing agricultural water use and yield \[[24](#9789815305210-C12.html#r24)\]. Agriculture uses a large percentage of the world's freshwater; hence, drip irrigation has been implemented to save water. Current systems, on the other hand, employ rubber or PVC tubing with minimal valve control, resulting in wasteful water consumption. To fine-tune water supply at the individual plant level, this research recommends retrofitting current systems with low-cost, passive, plastic screw-adjustable emitters. The design incorporates a mechatronic device for automatic field changes by human and robot teams, improving water supply precision. Experiments with a prototype show that it can compensate for orientation errors of up to 39 degrees and position errors of up to 42.5mm, offering a potential solution to optimize water resource utilization in agriculture \[[25](#9789815305210-C12.html#r25)\]. Traditional agricultural practices are proving insufficient, prompting the introduction of agro automation, notably Artificial Intelligence (AI). Artificial intelligence (AI) is transforming the sector by tackling concerns such as climate change, population expansion, employment, and food security. This research looks at a variety of AI applications in agriculture, including sensor-based irrigation, robotic weeding, and drone-assisted spraying. These improvements conserve resources, improve labor efficiency, boost production, and improve crop quality. The report examines the efforts of field researchers, with a particular emphasis on robotic weeding systems and the use of drones for crop monitoring and accurate spraying \[[26](#9789815305210-C12.html#r26)\]. Agriculture's vital position in a country's economy is being threatened by a growing population, changing weather, and resource restrictions. Precision agriculture is developing as an innovative solution powered by machine learning (ML) and IoT technologies in agricultural machinery. This article provides an in-depth look at machine learning applications in agriculture, including soil parameter prediction, crop yield calculation, disease and weed detection, species identification, and crop picture categorization using computer vision. The application of ML extends to livestock production, assisting in fertility prediction, diagnosing eating disorders, and interpreting cow behavior using collar sensors. Furthermore, the study investigates intelligent irrigation and harvesting systems, which reduce the need for human labor while emphasizing how knowledge-based agriculture may improve long-term production and product quality \[[27](#9789815305210-C12.html#r27)\]. Based on recent research, this article presents an up-to-date account of the evolution and current state of precision agricultural technology. Natural resource variability, variability management, management zones, the impact of precision agriculture on farm profitability and the environment, engineering innovations in sensors and remote sensing, information management, global applications, adoption trends, and the potential role of these technologies in modernizing agriculture in China are all covered \[[28](#9789815305210-C12.html#r28)\]. A precision agricultural system that combines sensor technology with a Farm Management Information System (FMIS) for irrigation and harvest management. The system is found to be economically acceptable in places with high irrigation demand due to low rainfall and unpredictable weather conditions, while its profitability is sensitive to fluctuations in fruit prices and capital costs. The study provides useful information for farmers and others contemplating precision farming approaches \[[29](#9789815305210-C12.html#r29)\]. The Agricultural Internet of Things (IoT) has significantly altered agricultural production by boosting productivity and product quality, lowering labor costs, and raising farmers' revenue. This study examines the current state of agricultural IoT by examining its system architecture, major technologies, and applications across several agricultural sectors. It also outlines current obstacles and projects for agricultural IoT's future growth, emphasizing its role in driving agricultural modernization and automation. Automated production management in horticulture provides a variety of practical applications that improve crop production efficiency, productivity, and sustainability \[[30](#9789815305210-C12.html#r30)\].

![](Images/9789815305210-C12-F2.jpg)**Fig. (2))**  
Technology applications in automated production management.  
**Source:** Prepared by the Author. 

The following are some of the most important practical uses of automated production management in horticulture:

### Precision Irrigation

Sensor data is used by automated systems to precisely distribute the needed amount of water to plants, optimizing water use and reducing over-irrigation. This guarantees that plants get the optimum amount of water at the right time, resulting in enhanced water efficiency and plant health.

### Robotic Crop Care

Sensors and cameras on robots and autonomous equipment conduct operations such as sowing, transplanting, weeding, and harvesting. These tools minimize labor requirements, improve accuracy, and boost operational efficiency, leading to better crop management and lower labor expenses.

### Climate Control in Controlled Environment Agriculture (CEA)

Automated systems in CEA facilities manage environmental elements such as temperature, humidity, CO[2] levels, and illumination. This allows for exact management of growing conditions and year-round cultivation, as well as optimized plant growth for greater output and quality.

### IPM (Integrated Pest Control)

Automated systems aid in pest and disease control by identifying early indicators of infestation and allowing for focused actions. Sensors and imaging technology aid in the detection of pest or disease outbreaks, allowing for prompt response to prevent crop loss and dependency on chemical treatments.

### Decision-Making Based on Data

Automated production management systems gather and analyze data from numerous sources, such as sensors, weather stations, and crop monitoring systems. This data-driven strategy delivers significant information to producers, allowing them to optimize crop management tactics, make educated decisions, and increase overall productivity and resource allocation. Automation systems accurately supply fertilizers, nutrients, and other inputs depending on crop requirements, minimizing waste and optimizing resource consumption. This results in economic savings, less environmental impact, and improved horticulture production sustainability.

Traceability and quality control are ensured by automated production management across the horticulture supply chain. It improves product integrity, decreases food safety hazards, and increases customer trust by correctly capturing and tracking data at each stage, from cultivation to post-harvest handling.

### Remote Monitoring and Control

Automation technologies enable horticultural processes to be monitored and managed remotely. Growers may access real-time data, get warnings, and regulate essential aspects such as irrigation, climatic conditions, and equipment functioning *via* mobile applications or web-based systems. This improves operational agility and efficiency. Automation decreases the need for human labor in repetitive and physically demanding jobs, freeing up labor for more skilled and strategic operations. This alleviates labor shortages, boosts overall efficiency, and increases horticulture yield. Automation systems optimize energy consumption in horticulture production by regulating lights in indoor farming or managing energy-intensive equipment. As a result, energy consumption is lowered, production costs are cut, and sustainability is improved. Automated production management solutions in horticulture give considerable benefits by increasing productivity, resource efficiency, and crop quality. These innovations increase horticulture enterprises' overall sustainability, profitability, and competitiveness by optimizing resource utilization and lowering dependency on human labor. Automated production management in horticulture includes the following main components:

### Robotic Systems

The use of robots and self-driving equipment to accomplish operations, including sowing, transplanting, harvesting, and trimming. These robots, which are equipped with sensors and actuators, travel around the crops, make precise motions, and perform specialized functions.

### Sensing and Monitoring

The installation of sensors and monitoring systems to collect real-time data on environmental variables like temperature, humidity, light levels, soil moisture, and nutrient content. This data enables data-driven decision-making and helps process modifications.

### Precision Irrigation

The use of automated irrigation systems to give exact amounts of water to plants based on sensor data. This allows effective water management in horticulture production, eliminates water waste, and assures optimal water consumption.

### Climate Control and Indoor Farming

Using automated climate control systems in controlled environment agriculture (CEA) or indoor farming. Temperature, humidity, CO[2] levels, and illumination are all controlled by these systems to provide an optimal growing environment for plants.

### Data Analytics and Decision Support

The analysis of acquired data using advanced data analytics techniques, machine learning algorithms, and prediction models. This gives information regarding crop health, growth trends, and yield estimates, allowing for better crop management decisions and increased production.

### Inventory Management and Resource Optimisation

Implementation of automated systems for inventory management, tracking input utilization (seeds, fertilizers, pesticides), and resource allocation optimization. This lowers waste, optimizes inventory levels, and ensures that resources are used efficiently.

### Task Scheduling and Workflows

The use of automation to schedule and manage different horticulture jobs and workflows. Planting, irrigation, fertilization, pest control, and harvest processes are all planned in order to maximizes labor utilization and minimize delays.

Integration and connectivity refer to the integration of various automated systems, technologies, and platforms in order to build a linked and unified production management environment. This allows for smooth communication, data sharing, and coordinated functioning of various components within the manufacturing system.

In horticulture, automated production management attempts to increase efficiency, minimize labor needs, improve crop quality, and optimizes resource utilization. Growers may attain increased productivity, accuracy, and sustainable practices in horticulture production by leveraging automation and modern technology.

## REVOLUTIONIZING HORTICULTURE: THE IMPACT OF AUTOMATION

Fig. ([**3**](#9789815305210-C12.html#f3)) shows that horticulture's future is set to see tremendous technical, environmental, and sociological breakthroughs. The increasing integration of precision agriculture, drones, and sensors in precision horticulture, promising resource optimization, and heightened efficiency through informed decision-making *via* big data and analytics are among the anticipated trends. Urban and vertical farming takes the front stage, emphasizing spatial optimization in urban environments to solve food security while minimizing food production's environmental imprint. Regulated environment agriculture, which includes vertical farms and hydroponics, is poised to thrive indoors under precisely regulated circumstances. Biotechnology and genetic engineering play an important role, with current research focused on genetically modified crops for higher production, pest and disease resistance, and nutritional characteristics. CRISPR tools help to create crops with certain desirable features. Climate-resilient crops are on the horizon, with an emphasis on cultivating varieties with improved drought resistance, heat tolerance, and resilience to extreme weather events. Sustainability is emphasized, with a drive for horticultural methods that reduce the impact of climate change on crop production. To minimize dependency on chemical pesticides, biological pest management options such as Integrated Pest Management (IPM) and the use of beneficial insects and biopesticides are gaining momentum. Microbial treatments, which use beneficial bacteria to decrease illness and increase nutrient intake, are emerging as viable options. Consumer-centric innovation manifests itself in the rising demand for horticulture items that provide health advantages beyond basic nourishment, resulting in greater development of medicinal specialty crops. Sensory appeal in crops, with emphasis on improved flavors, textures, and looks, accommodates changing customer preferences. Smart farming and automation are transforming traditional agricultural techniques by using robots and automation for operations such as planting, harvesting, and weeding, lowering reliance on physical labor and increasing overall efficiency. The future of horticulture is dynamic, with an emphasis on sustainability, technology convergence, and proactive answers to the difficulties provided by a changing climate and a growing global population. Collaboration between academics, practitioners, and politicians is critical to realizing these future routes, which will benefit both producers and consumers.

![](Images/9789815305210-C12-F3.jpg)**Fig. (3))**  
Revolutionizing Horticulture: The Impact of Automation.  
**Source:** Prepared by the Author. 

## HORTICULTURL TECHNIQUES: TRADITIONAL *VS*. MODERN

Fig. ([**4**](#9789815305210-C12.html#f4)), related to traditional horticulture practices, includes time-honored techniques for growing fruits, vegetables, herbs, and decorative plants. These strategies, which are based on a deep understanding of local ecosystems, climatic variations, and varied plant kinds, continue to be important components of agricultural systems. Despite the advances achieved by current horticultural technology, the continued significance of conventional ways is obvious, especially in countries that place a priority on sustainable and established methodologies. Crop rotation stands out among these methods, involving the sequential cultivation of various crops to reduce soil erosion, manage pests and diseases, and maintain soil fertility. Furthermore, by producing various crops concurrently, polyculture and mixed cropping boost biodiversity, improve pest management, and optimize resource utilization. Agroforestry, a traditional approach, involves the integration of trees and shrubs with crops to provide benefits such as shade, windbreaks, extra food sources, and better soil fertility. Terracing, which is used in steep terrains, avoids soil erosion, conserves water, and allows for more effective land usage. Another option is companion planting, which includes intentionally planting crops together to boost growth and defend against pests, such as combining basil with tomatoes. Traditional farmers help to preserve indigenous plant kinds by practicing seed saving and storing seeds from one harvest for the following planting season. Natural fertilizers, such as manure and compost, are used extensively in traditional horticulture to improve soil structure and provide critical nutrients. While not precisely traditional, drip irrigation, which has been used for centuries in some areas, reduces water waste by delivering water directly to plant roots. Heritage cultivars, which are often farmed, are passed down through generations because to their adaptability and attractive characteristics. Many traditional horticulturists use manual cultivation and harvesting methods, such as hand tilling and weeding, which are particularly sustainable in smaller-scale enterprises. Finally, communities' essential indigenous knowledge and local wisdom give insights into native flora, local growth conditions, and sustainable methods passed down through generations.

![](Images/9789815305210-C12-F4.jpg)**Fig. (4))**  
Horticultural Techniques: Traditional *vs*. Modern.  
**Source:** Prepared by the Author. 

Advanced technology and scientific approaches intersect in modern horticulture to optimize plant development, increase yields, and improve resource efficiency. To fulfil these objectives, a variety of current procedures have been created. Hydroponics, a soilless growing approach, allows plants to thrive in nutrient-rich water solutions while providing perfect environmental control. Vertical farming, which involves growing plants in stacked layers in controlled indoor conditions, maximizes area utilization and enables year-round production. Precision agriculture optimizes farm management by using GPS, sensors, and data analytics to administer resources such as water, fertilizer, and pesticides based on real-time data. Greenhouse horticulture provides a controlled environment that extends growth seasons and protects against inclement weather. To generate crops with specified features, biotechnology and genetic engineering use tools such as CRISPR for precise gene editing. Precision irrigation, which employs techniques like drip irrigation, reduces water waste while increasing efficiency. Satellite imagery and drones, for example, monitor crops on a vast scale, allowing for early diagnosis of problems. For monitoring and decision-making, smart sensors and the Internet of Things (IoT) collect real-time data on environmental conditions. Integrated Pest Management includes biological pest management, which reduces the need for chemical pesticides. Planting and harvesting processes are becoming more automated and robotic, lowering labor expenses. Geoponics and aquaponics are soilless growth methods that maximize resource utilization. Biostimulants promote nutrient absorption, while LED lighting systems customized to plant development phases save electricity. Large datasets are processed by data analytics and AI for predictive modeling and decision assistance. The use of these techniques in modern horticulture results in efficient, sustainable, and high-yielding agricultural systems that fulfill the needs of the world's rising population.

## FINDINGS OF THE STUDY

The research on automated production management in horticulture outlines a paradigm transition from Industry 1.0 to Industry 4.0, demonstrating significant technical advances in cultivation and management methods. The use of automated technology such as robotic harvesting, sensor-based monitoring, and precision irrigation, results in significant increases in product quality, improved crop production, lower labor costs, and decreased environmental effects. Among the notable themes mentioned are the use of artificial intelligence and machine learning for better decision-making. However, obstacles such as costly initial investment, technological complexity, and a shortage of experienced workers are recognized. The study emphasizes the potential for real-time monitoring of data analytics, remote sensing, and IoT technologies. Future paths will emphasize tackling difficulties through cost-effective solutions and increased access to technology. For the flexibility and sustainability of automated horticulture systems, crop diversity, growth phases, and environmental variables are emphasized. While problems remain, adopting automated production management appears to be a potential option for horticulture stakeholders to succeed in a competitive and resource-constrained global context. Traditional horticulture approaches, which are firmly established in the understanding of local ecosystems and sustainability, coexist with modern advances. Crop rotation, polyculture, and agroforestry are all practices that help with biodiversity, pest management, and resource efficiency. Traditional farming methods emphasize manual cultivation, seed saving, and natural fertilizers, with an emphasis on sustainability and the preservation of native plant species. Modern horticulture, on the other hand, uses cutting-edge technologies like hydroponics, vertical farming, precision agriculture, and biotechnology to improve plant growth, resource utilization, and total productivity. The combination of traditional and contemporary technologies constitutes a holistic strategy for developing efficient, sustainable, and high-yielding agricultural systems capable of satisfying the demands of a growing global population.

## RECOMMENDATIONS OF THE STUDY

The report offers detailed recommendations for efficiently blending traditional and modern horticulture methods. To overcome this gap, it advocates the use of holistic agricultural practices that combine traditional knowledge with modern technologies, with an emphasis on a balanced and sustainable agriculture plan. It is recommended to increase collaboration between traditional and modern practitioners by facilitating knowledge transfer through projects and training programmes, creating a reciprocal exchange of abilities. Improving technology access, preserving indigenous plant types, and enacting supporting regulations are all necessary steps toward creating an inclusive agricultural ecosystem. Investing in R&D, rewarding sustainable practices, and encouraging community participation and education are all critical for developing a resilient horticulture industry that capitalizes on the benefits of both old and modern approaches. The report also emphasizes the need to form farmer cooperatives, support agroecological concepts, and manage post-harvest losses through improved storage and transportation networks. Creating discussion and knowledge-sharing platforms allows continual learning and reciprocal inspiration between traditional and modern practitioners. Finally, supporting efforts focused on agricultural education and vocational training emerge as a critical strategy for equipping the next generation of farmers with the necessary skills for navigating the developing horticultural landscape. Given the research's potential consequences, a series of suggestions arise to direct horticulture's future towards sustainability and technical progress. First and foremost, there is a demand for more investment in R&D, notably in precision agriculture, biotechnology, and the creation of climate-resilient crop types. To maintain continuous innovation in the industry, government agencies, research institutes, and business partners must work together. Incentives for farmers to integrate robots and automation into horticulture operations should be a focus for encouraging the use of smart farming methods and automation technology. Policymakers are critical in developing frameworks that encourage the use of sustainable and climate-resilient horticulture techniques. To create a trained workforce capable of navigating the future horticulture environment educational activities should focus on teaching the next generation of horticulturists in innovative technology and sustainable practices. Finally, continual cooperation and knowledge-sharing platforms are critical for facilitating the interchange of ideas and innovations among academics, practitioners, and policymakers, supporting a collaborative effort towards a resilient, technologically advanced, and sustainable future for horticulture.

## CONCLUSION

The study foresees a bright future for horticulture, characterized by technological improvements, sustainability, and responsiveness to social requirements. Precision agriculture, drones, and sensors are being integrated into horticulture operations to optimize resources and improve efficiency through data-driven decision-making. Urban and vertical farming, as well as controlled environment agriculture, are poised to address food security while minimizing environmental impact, while biotechnology, including genetic engineering and tools such as CRISPR, holds the promise of improved crop yield, pest resistance, and nutritional profiles. Prioritizing climate-resilient crops and sustainable methods demonstrates a dedication to reducing the effects of climate change on agricultural output. The growing emphasis on biological pest management, microbial solutions, and consumer-focused innovation indicates a trend toward more sustainable and health-conscious horticulture. The use of smart farming and automation is projected to transform traditional methods by lowering human labor and increasing overall efficiency. Collaboration among researchers, practitioners, and policymakers has been acknowledged as critical to achieving these future paths. To navigate the shifting horticultural environment, proactive, coordinated efforts are required to exploit technology breakthroughs, promote sustainability, and successfully manage the difficulties provided by a changing climate and a growing global population.
